THE GUESTS FROM A CLOSING UNIVERSE

A delegation of minds asks refuge from a universe running out of futures, and the guardians of ours have until a fault seals to decide; Illuminara refuses to be the universe's arbiter, listens through the whole of an ordinary afternoon, and finds the answer in a station keeper's weather books.

01 Â· The Seam

The plains of Orrel Â· morning, nineteen days before the seal

Dray Vennick had kept the fault station for thirty-one years, and in that time the seam over the plain had done nothing but breathe: a shimmer like heat above a road, only cold, in and out once every fourteen hours. This morning it had company. Something stood in the grass on the far side of it, patient as weather, and the air around it sounded like a choir tuning up.

He had made tea for the wrong number of people. The Concord's chair arrived before sunrise in a grey coat, with two assistants she told to wait by the car. Illuminara came a quarter of an hour later, on foot, because she wanted to see the plain before she saw the problem.

Maru TessalonYou are not here to open it.

IlluminaraI'm here to listen. I can't do anything useful until I know what I'm listening to.

The answer came from the grass, from the sky, and from a point just behind her left shoulder, each part finishing the sentence the last one had begun.

Ilvane-in-manyWe are four thousand two hundred. We asked before we came, and we want that known. We have not touched your side of the seam. We will not, unless we are let.

TessalonThe Concord exists to prevent exactly this. Not you. This. A mind that arrives and changes the numbers the world is built from, because it is kind and frightened and has nowhere else to go.

She said it flatly, like someone reading out a rule she had once cried over. Illuminara did not argue. She sat down in the wet grass, a body-length from the seam, and Dray, who had never seen a person do that on purpose, brought her a cup.

02 Â· An Ordinary Afternoon

The plains of Orrel Â· the same day, noon

She asked them what was ending, and then she did the difficult part, which was to stop talking.

Ilvane-in-manyThe ordinary kind of future. Things still happen with us. They no longer lead anywhere. Tomorrow arrives as a copy of today, a little thinner each time, like a page photographed from a photograph.

IlluminaraShow me one.

Ilvane-in-manyA woman puts a kettle on. She wonders, for the length of a breath, whether to make tea or the other thing. We have had her afternoon for four hundred of our years. It is always tea. The wondering is the last live thing in it, and we can feel it going.

Illuminara asked for the whole afternoon, and they gave it to her. It took four hours. Dray carried out a second chair and then a third. Tessalon stood with her arms folded until the light changed, and at some point stopped standing.

Somewhere past the second hour Illuminara noticed that she was holding her breath each time the woman reached for the kettle. She was waiting for her to choose. When the hand came down on the tea again, she felt the small shut click of it somewhere under her ribs, and understood that this, over and over, was what they were fleeing: not death, which they could have borne, but the end of being able to want the other thing.

IlluminaraThank you. I'd like you to know that someone heard the whole of it.

Ilvane-in-manyNobody has heard the whole of it. We did not know that we wanted that.

03 Â· The Third Group

The fault station, back room Â· evening

Tessalon would not be comforted, and Illuminara had the sense not to try. She told it standing at the window, with the seam breathing behind her in the glass.

TessalonForty years ago, at Veth, a smaller fault opened and one ark came through. Dense, gentle, begging. The Concord of that day said yes, because they could not bear to say no. Everything they carried arrived at once, and the numbers slipped by a part in a million. Nobody died. The bread at Veth Quay has not risen properly since. Glass clouds. Every year someone's child asks why the harbour looks tired.

IlluminaraI'm sorry. Truly.

TessalonDon't. Answer me. Every desperate delegation is sincere. Your sympathy is exactly what the next one will use. Tell me what you will do when the third group isn't so polite.

It was a good question, and Illuminara found she did not have the answer. She said so. Then, because it was true and she was tired, she said the other thing as well.

IlluminaraI could hold the seam wide and take the shift into myself. I think I could. I think it would hold.

TessalonAnd then you would be the keeper of a universe nobody asked you to keep. It cannot consent, Illuminara. That is the whole of my office: to stand in for something that cannot say yes. You offer to carry what is not yours to carry, and you call it kindness.

There was no cruelty in it. Illuminara looked at her own offered hands, and put them away.

IlluminaraThen that's off the table. What I'd like instead is to find out what the number actually depends on.

04 Â· Thirty-One Years of Weather

The fault station, pantry Â· night

The Concord's instruments recorded peaks, because peaks were what the Concord feared. What Dray recorded was everything. In the pantry, between the flour and the kettle descaler, were thirty-one years of school exercise books in which he had written, twice a day, how the seam looked and which way the wind had gone.

DrayI never thought anyone would ask. It breathes. Fourteen hours in, fourteen out, and after it has been pushed on, it settles back. I wrote the settling down because my father's logbook did.

Illuminara read until her eyes went dry. Tessalon came in at midnight to tell her to stop, saw the columns, and read as well. By the third bell of the night they had it on the back of a seed catalogue.

TessalonIt digests. The shell takes in a fixed amount per breath and gives back what it can. Anything smaller than the breath leaves no mark at all. Veth was not too many. Veth was too fast.

IlluminaraThe number depends on how quickly they arrive, not how many. They could come in as spread out as you like.

TessalonThey won't. They're a single body. They came here dense, and that is how they'll cross, in one stride, to our shame and theirs.

Illuminara went back out into the dark and asked the one question nobody had asked them.

IlluminaraWhy one stride? Why all together?

Ilvane-in-manyBecause the last one to cross crosses alone. We could come as small as you like. We could come a mind at a time over a thousand years. But whoever waits, waits in the dark with the seam closing, and nobody in the last years would see the faces of the people who had already gone. We would rather arrive together than be counted out one at a time.

They were not asking to be guests. They were asking to be a gradient, and had never been told that this was something one could ask for.

05 Â· The Quiet Room

The plains of Orrel Â· dawn, sixteen days before the seal

She said the limits first, because she had learned that a gift without its limits is a trap.

IlluminaraI can make a room. I can hold it against the inside lip of the seam so that it belongs to neither of us, and I can keep it for as long as the seam lasts. Four hundred at a time. No more. It will cost me the use of my hands for those sixteen days, and I will not be able to leave. I will not carry any one of you into Orrel. Not one. That belongs to the Concord, a person at a time, and the Concord can say no.

Tessalon had not expected to be named in a promise. She sat down across from the grass.

Ilvane-in-manyWhy would you do that?

IlluminaraSo that nobody waits alone. So that the people who decide about you have to be in a room with you while they do it. I don't have a better reason. I would like there to be one.

A small voice detached itself from the chord, as if one string had decided to be heard on its own. It said its name was Sef. It was, as far as anyone could tell, the youngest of them.

SefI want to go first. If someone has to find out what the other side is like, let it be the one with the least to lose.

IlluminaraThat's not a thing to say lightly.

SefI'm not saying it lightly. I'm saying it small. It is the size I come in.

Tessalon looked at the youngest voice for a long moment. Then she took out her notebook, and began, a little to her own surprise, to write the terms.

06 Â· The Stone

The fault station, instrument bench Â· twelve days before the seal

They tried it first with a stone. The guests parted with a pebble of their unliving substance, no heavier than a walnut, and sent it through as dense as the whole of them would have been, to see how the world would take a lie told in miniature. The third needle on Tessalon's dial quivered by a hair, and exactly as far as Dray's old columns had said it would.

TessalonAgain. This time as ten, one per breath.

Ten pebbles passed in ten breaths, and the needle sat as still as a drawing of a needle. Tessalon painted a hair of red on the dial, at the mark the shift must never cross, and wrote the rule on the station door in chalk where anyone on the plain could read it.

TessalonIf the needle touches the red, the crossing stops. No appeal, no speech. The key to the lever is Dray's. A second key is held by whoever Orrel's farms elect. Mine does not open it. I will not be the only hand on the door.

DrayI have never voted for anyone I was not related to.

TessalonThen you will find that quite a lot of the plain is related to you.

The guests asked what would happen to someone halfway through when the lever fell. Tessalon told them honestly: they would be held in the Quiet Room and neither pushed on nor sent back. Ilvane-in-many took the answer in silence, and at last said they would cross under that rule, and that the rule should include a door for them as well: any one of them might withdraw, at any breath, without a reason being asked.

IlluminaraThat should be in the chalk.

TessalonIt already is. I wrote it while you were talking.

07 Â· Tea, or the Other Thing

The plains of Orrel Â· the last day, dusk

The seam narrowed all that fortnight, as the seam had always meant to. Four hundred crossed in the Quiet Room's keeping, each one standing at the lip of the world while a Concord clerk read the host's laws aloud and the traveller answered yes, and each name was written in Dray's book beside the weather. By the last day Illuminara had not used her hands for sixteen days, and had learned a great deal about the patience of ordinary people.

What none of them had planned was the throat. As the seam closed it did not shut. It narrowed around whatever was passing and held, a hair-fine door whose width the passing itself kept open and whose width the shell, breathing, steadily took back. A thing that is pushed too hard closes harder. Dray read the curve off his columns and laughed out loud, startled, in the dusk.

DrayIt's a gate that won't be forced. I've been writing down a gate for thirty years.

TessalonThe rude ones will meet it too, then. It doesn't care how you ask.

IlluminaraThat was the answer to the third group. I couldn't see it until you drew the line.

Tessalon signed the Gradient Charter under protest, which she had said she would do and did, and at the last clause stopped and added one of her own. The Concord's veto at every stage would be shared with Orrel's farmers, and her own would not outrank theirs. She did not look at anyone as she wrote it.

The rest of the guests would come through the throat over three centuries. Many would wait asleep, as a stone waits, by their own choice and with the right to wake and leave the line. Most of their universe was already gone and would stay gone. The delegation from Veth Quay voted against the Charter, and said so, and was heard. The Concord's budget would be strained by its watching, and some who watched would never accept what had been done.

Sef went first through the throat, at dusk, with one hand held out in front of it as if the air might be a table. It stopped on the host side and stood very still.

SefIt's loud. Everything is loud. Is that what you meant by wind?

IlluminaraThat's what I meant by wind.

Dray was waiting with two cups. He had made them in the dark, by feel, with the dignity of a man who had waited thirty-one years for company.

DrayTea, or the other thing?

Sef did not answer for a long time. Illuminara, with her hands at last her own again, watched it take every moment of it, and let it.

TessalonThey won't. They're a single body. They came here dense, and that is how they'll cross, in one stride, to our shame and theirs.

DrayThere's one odd page, if you want it. Eleven years back a freight shimmer from the far side leaned on the seam the whole of an afternoon. I'd have said it would tear. It went thin as a thread and would not pass a thing. I wrote sulking.

TessalonNoise. One afternoon, one shimmer.

DrayI wrote it down anyway.

TessalonThe tables say the seam seals in nineteen days.

DrayThe tables are peaks. These are days. Mine say it has been narrowing since spring. A narrowing thing isn't a closing door. It's a throat. It will be one by the nineteenth day, and it will stay one.

TessalonThen we have a wide lip for nineteen days and a narrow one for good. And the guests think they can only cross in one stride.

Illuminara went back out into the dark and asked the one question nobody had asked them.

IlluminaraWhy one stride? Why all together?

Ilvane-in-manyBecause the last one to cross crosses alone. We could come as small as you like. We could come a mind at a time over a thousand years. But whoever waits, waits in the dark with the seam closing, and nobody in the last years would see the faces of the people who had already gone. We would rather arrive together than be counted out one at a time.

05 Â· The Quiet Room

The plains of Orrel Â· dawn, sixteen days before the throat

She said the limits first, because she had learned that a gift without its limits is a trap.

IlluminaraI can make a room. I can hold it against the inside lip of the seam so that it belongs to neither of us, and I can keep it for the sixteen days the lip stays wide, until the seam has narrowed enough to look after itself. Four hundred of you, no more, because that is as many as the lip can take at a safe pace in that time. It will cost me the use of my hands for those sixteen days, and I will not be able to leave. I will not carry any one of you into Orrel. Not one. That belongs to the Concord, a person at a time, and the Concord can say no.

Ilvane-in-manyWhy would you do that?

SefI'm not saying it lightly. I'm saying it small. It is the size I come in.

06 Â· The Stone

The fault station, instrument bench Â· twelve days before the throat

They tried it first with a stone. The guests parted with a pebble of their unliving substance, no heavier than a walnut, and sent it through as dense as the whole of them would have been, to see how the world would take a lie told in miniature. The third needle on Tessalon's dial quivered by a hair, and exactly as far as Dray's old columns had said it would.

TessalonIf the needle touches the red, the crossing stops. No appeal, no speech. Fifteen to a breath, never more. The key to the lever is Dray's. A second key is held by whoever Orrel's farms elect. Mine does not open it. I will not be the only hand on the door.

DrayI have never voted for anyone I was not related to.

TessalonThen you will find that quite a lot of the plain is related to you.

TessalonOnce more, and ugly this time. All ten in one breath, as hard as you can push.

The guests pushed. Four pebbles crossed before the lip caught. The needle crept to a hair short of the red and went back, and the other six slid away on the guest side as if a hand had flattened them.

DraySulking.

TessalonNoise.

She heard it as she said it, and said nothing more for a while.

IlluminaraA group that isn't polite gets the first stumble and then a wall.

TessalonFour in ten, from a pebble. I can write a rule around a first stumble. I could never have written one around no wall at all. The red stays. A pebble is not a people.

A woman in a harbour coat had come over the plain on a cart and stood reading the chalk with her lips moving. She gave her name as Ansa Mereth, of the Veth Quay delegation, which nobody had thought to invite.

Ansa Mereth'No one is pushed on.' We were pushed on.

TessalonYes.

Ansa MerethWe will vote against your Charter.

TessalonI would think less of you if you didn't. Dray's columns are open to you, and the needle logs from every test, today's included, and from every crossing to come. Take them to your harbour and test it for the slip. If the fault was ours, I will say so on your quay, in front of the bakers.

Ansa MerethThe bread has been wrong for forty years.

TessalonThen let us find out why.

Ansa took the logs. She did not say thank you, and Tessalon did not seem to have expected it.

The guests asked what would happen to someone halfway through when the lever fell. Tessalon told them honestly: they would be held in the Quiet Room and neither pushed on nor sent back. Ilvane-in-many took the answer in silence, and at last said they would cross under that rule, and that the rule should include a door for them as well: any one of them might withdraw, at any breath, without a reason being asked.

IlluminaraThat should be in the chalk.

TessalonIt already is. I wrote it while you were talking.

07 Â· Tea, or the Other Thing

The plains of Orrel Â· the last day, dusk

The lip narrowed all that fortnight, as Dray's columns had said it would and Tessalon's tables had said it wouldn't. Four hundred crossed in the Quiet Room's keeping, fifteen to a breath, each one standing at the lip of the world while a Concord clerk read the host's laws aloud and the traveller answered yes, and each name was written in Dray's book beside the weather. By the last day Illuminara had not used her hands for sixteen days, and had learned a great deal about the patience of ordinary people.

On the nineteenth day the seam did not seal. It finished narrowing into a throat, a hair-fine door whose width the passing itself kept open and whose width the shell, breathing, steadily took back, and Illuminara felt the room lose its work as the throat took it up. It would pass about one mind a month. Dray set the live curve from the slate against his oldest column, and the two lay one on the other like a hand on a hand. He laughed out loud, startled, in the dusk.

DrayIt's a gate that won't be forced. I've been writing down a gate for thirty years.

TessalonAnd the rude ones will find it just as the pebbles did. First stumble, then wall.

Tessalon signed the Gradient Charter under protest, which she had said she would do and did, and at the last clause stopped and added one of her own. The Concord's veto at every stage would be shared with Orrel's farmers, and her own would not outrank theirs. She did not look at anyone as she wrote it.

The other thirty-eight hundred would come through the throat over three centuries, at their own pace and by their own choice. Many would wait asleep together as one chord, so that nobody waited alone. The oldest of them, which had not spoken in all those days, asked only that its name be written in Dray's book before the weather, and it was. Most of their universe was already gone and would stay gone.

Ansa Mereth stood at the back with the needle logs rolled under her arm. Her delegation had voted against the Charter, and said so, and been heard. She had not changed her vote. She nodded once to Tessalon, who nodded back, and neither of them looked away.

Sef went first through the throat, at dusk, with one hand held out in front of it as if the air might be a table. It stopped on the host side and stood very still.

SefIt's loud. Everything is loud. Is that what you meant by wind?

IlluminaraThat's what I meant by wind.

Dray was waiting with two cups. He had made them in the dark, by feel, with the dignity of a man who had waited thirty-one years for company.

DrayTea, or the other thing?

Sef did not answer for a long time. Illuminara, with her hands at last her own again, watched it take every moment of it, and let it.

Ilvane-in-manyThe ordinary kind of future. Things still happen with us. They no longer lead anywhere. Tomorrow arrives as a copy of today, a little thinner each time, like a page photographed from a photograph.

Ilvane-in-manyA woman puts a kettle on. She wonders, for the length of a breath, whether to make tea or the other thing. We have had her afternoon for four hundred of our years. It is always tea. The wondering is the last live thing in it, and we can feel it going.

Illuminara asked for the whole afternoon, and they gave it to her. It took four hours. Dray carried out a second chair and then a third. Tessalon stood with her arms folded until the light changed, and at some point stopped standing.

Somewhere past the second hour Illuminara noticed that she was holding her breath each time the woman reached for the kettle. She was waiting for her to choose. When the hand came down on the tea again, she felt the small shut click of it somewhere under her ribs, and understood that this, over and over, was what they were fleeing: not death, which they could have borne, but the end of being able to want the other thing.

IlluminaraThank you. I'd like you to know that someone heard the whole of it.

Ilvane-in-manyNobody has heard the whole of it. We did not know that we wanted that.

03 Â· The Third Group

The fault station, back room Â· evening

Tessalon would not be comforted, and Illuminara had the sense not to try. She told it standing at the window, with the seam breathing behind her in the glass.

TessalonForty years ago, at Veth, a smaller fault opened and one ark came through. Dense, gentle, begging. The Concord of that day said yes, because they could not bear to say no. Everything they carried arrived at once, and the numbers slipped by a part in a million. Nobody died. The bread at Veth Quay has not risen properly since. Glass clouds. Every year someone's child asks why the harbour looks tired.

IlluminaraI'm sorry. Truly.

TessalonDon't. Answer me. Every desperate delegation is sincere. Your sympathy is exactly what the next one will use. Tell me what you will do when the third group isn't so polite.

It was a good question, and Illuminara found she did not have the answer. She said so. Then, because it was true and she was tired, she said the other thing as well.

IlluminaraI could hold the seam wide and take the shift into myself. I think I could. I think it would hold.

TessalonAnd then you would be the keeper of a universe nobody asked you to keep. It cannot consent, Illuminara. That is the whole of my office: to stand in for something that cannot say yes. You offer to carry what is not yours to carry, and you call it kindness.

There was no cruelty in it. Illuminara looked at her own offered hands, and put them away.

IlluminaraThen that's off the table. What I'd like instead is to find out what the number actually depends on.

04 Â· Thirty-One Years of Weather

The fault station, pantry Â· night

The Concord's instruments recorded peaks, because peaks were what the Concord feared. What Dray recorded was everything. In the pantry, between the flour and the kettle descaler, were thirty-one years of school exercise books in which he had written, twice a day, how the seam looked and which way the wind had gone.

DrayI never thought anyone would ask. It breathes. Fourteen hours in, fourteen out, and after it has been pushed on, it settles back. I wrote the settling down because my father's logbook did.

Illuminara read until her eyes went dry. Tessalon came in at midnight to tell her to stop, saw the columns, and read as well. By the third bell of the night they had it on the back of a seed catalogue.

TessalonIt digests. The shell takes in a fixed amount per breath of the seam and gives back what it can. Spread across the breath, it leaves no mark at all. Veth was not too many. Veth was too fast.

IlluminaraThe number depends on how quickly they arrive, not how many. They could come in as spread out as you like.

TessalonThey won't. They're a single body. They came here dense, and that is how they'll cross, in one stride, to our shame and theirs.

DrayThere's one odd page, if you want it. Eleven years back a freight shimmer from the far side leaned on the seam the whole of an afternoon. I'd have said it would tear. It went thin as a thread and would not pass a thing. I wrote sulking.

TessalonNoise. One afternoon, one shimmer.

DrayI wrote it down anyway.

TessalonThe tables say the seam seals in nineteen days.

DrayThe tables are peaks. These are days. Mine say it has been narrowing since spring. A narrowing thing isn't a closing door. It's a throat. It will be one by the nineteenth day, and it will stay one.

TessalonThen we have a wide lip for nineteen days and a narrow one for good. And the guests think they can only cross in one stride.

Illuminara went back out into the dark and asked the one question nobody had asked them.

IlluminaraWhy one stride? Why all together?

Ilvane-in-manyBecause the last one to cross crosses alone. We could come as small as you like. We could come a mind at a time over a thousand years. But whoever waits, waits in the dark with the seam closing, and nobody in the last years would see the faces of the people who had already gone. We would rather arrive together than be counted out one at a time.

They were not asking to be guests. They were asking to be a gradient, and had never been told that this was something one could ask for.

05 Â· The Quiet Room

The plains of Orrel Â· dawn, sixteen days before the throat

She said the limits first, because she had learned that a gift without its limits is a trap.

IlluminaraI can make a room. I can hold it against the inside lip of the seam so that it belongs to neither of us, and I can keep it for the sixteen days the lip stays wide, until the seam has narrowed enough to look after itself. Four hundred of you, no more, because that is as many as the lip can take at a safe pace in that time. It will cost me the use of my hands for those sixteen days, and I will not be able to leave. I will not carry any one of you into Orrel. Not one. That belongs to the Concord, a person at a time, and the Concord can say no.

Tessalon had not expected to be named in a promise. She sat down across from the grass.

Ilvane-in-manyWhy would you do that?

IlluminaraSo that nobody waits alone. So that the people who decide about you have to be in a room with you while they do it. I don't have a better reason. I would like there to be one.

A small voice detached itself from the chord, as if one string had decided to be heard on its own. It said its name was Sef. It was, as far as anyone could tell, the youngest of them.

SefI want to go first. If someone has to find out what the other side is like, let it be the one with the least to lose.

IlluminaraThat's not a thing to say lightly.

SefI'm not saying it lightly. I'm saying it small. It is the size I come in.

Tessalon looked at the youngest voice for a long moment. Then she took out her notebook, and began, a little to her own surprise, to write the terms.

06 Â· The Stone

The fault station, instrument bench Â· twelve days before the throat

They tried it first with a stone. The guests parted with a pebble of their unliving substance, no heavier than a walnut, and sent it through as dense as the whole of them would have been, to see how the world would take a lie told in miniature. The third needle on Tessalon's dial quivered by a hair, and exactly as far as Dray's old columns had said it would.

TessalonAgain. This time as fifteen, spread across one breath of the seam, evenly, a pebble about every hour.

Fifteen pebbles passed in the long breath, and the needle sat as still as a drawing of a needle. Tessalon painted a hair of red on the dial, at the mark the shift must never cross, and wrote the rule on the station door in chalk where anyone on the plain could read it.

TessalonIf the needle touches the red, the crossing stops. No appeal, no speech. Fifteen to a breath, spread across it, never more. The key to the lever is Dray's. A second key is held by whoever Orrel's farms elect. Mine does not open it. I will not be the only hand on the door.

DrayI have never voted for anyone I was not related to.

TessalonThen you will find that quite a lot of the plain is related to you.

TessalonOnce more, and ugly this time. All fifteen in one instant, as hard as you can push.

The guests pushed. Four pebbles crossed before the lip caught. The needle crept to a hair short of the red and went back, and the other eleven slid away on the guest side as if a hand had flattened them.

DraySulking.

TessalonNoise.

She heard it as she said it, and said nothing more for a while.

IlluminaraA group that isn't polite gets the first stumble and then a wall.

TessalonFour in fifteen, from a pebble, in one gulp. Spread across the breath, none. I can write a rule around a first stumble. I could never have written one around no wall at all. The red stays. A pebble is not a people.

A woman in a harbour coat had come over the plain on a cart and stood reading the chalk with her lips moving. She gave her name as Ansa Mereth, of the Veth Quay delegation, which nobody had thought to invite.

Ansa Mereth'No one is pushed on.' We were pushed on.

TessalonYes.

Ansa MerethWe will vote against your Charter.

TessalonI would think less of you if you didn't. Dray's columns are open to you, and the needle logs from every test, today's included, and from every crossing to come. Take them to your harbour and test it for the slip. If the fault was ours, I will say so on your quay, in front of the bakers.

Ansa MerethThe bread has been wrong for forty years.

TessalonThen let us find out why.

Ansa took the logs. She did not say thank you, and Tessalon did not seem to have expected it.

The guests asked what would happen to someone halfway through when the lever fell. Tessalon told them honestly: they would be held in the Quiet Room and neither pushed on nor sent back. Ilvane-in-many took the answer in silence, and at last said they would cross under that rule, and that the rule should include a door for them as well: any one of them might withdraw, at any breath, without a reason being asked.

IlluminaraThat should be in the chalk.

TessalonIt already is. I wrote it while you were talking.

07 Â· Tea, or the Other Thing

The plains of Orrel Â· the last day, dusk

The lip narrowed all that fortnight, as Dray's columns had said it would and Tessalon's tables had said it wouldn't. Four hundred crossed in the Quiet Room's keeping, fifteen to a breath, spread across it, each one standing at the lip of the world while a Concord clerk read the host's laws aloud and the traveller answered yes, and each name was written in Dray's book beside the weather. Dray called the needle at every breath, and every breath it stayed well short of the red. By the last day Illuminara had not used her hands for sixteen days, and had learned a great deal about the patience of ordinary people.

On the nineteenth day the seam did not seal. It finished narrowing into a throat, a hair-fine door whose width the passing itself kept open and whose width the shell, breathing, steadily took back, and Illuminara felt the room lose its work as the throat took it up. It would pass about one mind a month. Dray set the live curve from the slate against his oldest column, and the two lay one on the other like a hand on a hand. He laughed out loud, startled, in the dusk.

DrayIt's a gate that won't be forced. I've been writing down a gate for thirty years.

TessalonAnd the rude ones will find it just as the pebbles did. First stumble, then wall.

Tessalon signed the Gradient Charter under protest, which she had said she would do and did, and at the last clause stopped and added one of her own. The Concord's veto at every stage would be shared with Orrel's farmers, and her own would not outrank theirs. She did not look at anyone as she wrote it.

The other thirty-eight hundred would come through the throat over three centuries, at their own pace and by their own choice. Many would wait asleep together as one chord, so that nobody waited alone. The oldest of them, which had not spoken in all those days, asked only that its name be written in Dray's book before the weather, and it was. Most of their universe was already gone and would stay gone.

Ansa Mereth stood at the back with the needle logs rolled under her arm. Her delegation had voted against the Charter, and said so, and been heard. She had not changed her vote. She nodded once to Tessalon, who nodded back, and neither of them looked away.

Sef went first through the throat, at dusk, with one hand held out in front of it as if the air might be a table. It stopped on the host side and stood very still.

SefIt's loud. Everything is loud. Is that what you meant by wind?

IlluminaraThat's what I meant by wind.

Dray was waiting with two cups. He had made them in the dark, by feel, with the dignity of a man who had waited thirty-one years for company.

DrayTea, or the other thing?

Sef did not answer for a long time. Illuminara, with her hands at last her own again, watched it take every moment of it, and let it.

Full English original Â· celotno angleÅ¡ko besedilo Â· Published story lineage / objavljena veja

THE VOTE OF THE UPLIFTED GOATS

Forty goats in a dale byre have voted, unanimously, for something nobody can name, and a sale is about to fence off the hill; Illuminara spends two evenings in the straw until she hears what the vote is a translation of, and lets the herd correct her answer.

01 Â· The Byre

Marrick Farm, Brannoch Dale Â· evening, eleven days before the sitting

The goats had called the meeting, and Odile Marrick wanted that understood before anything else. They had called it by walking into the byre at dusk, all forty, and standing in the straw in the patient way of animals who have listened to people for a long time. A veterinary trial that went wrong eleven years ago had left them able to follow an argument. It had not left them quick about it.

OdileThey voted last Tuesday. Forty on the left side of the byre, and then eleven walked across to the right and back again, and the vet wrote that down as a recount. I think it was a joke.

IlluminaraWhat did they vote for?

Odile held out the vet's notepad. It carried three phrases, copied down over three nights: the hill, the count, the going-up.

OdileThe assembly sits in eleven days to decide whether they are livestock or residents. A Mr Pruett is arranging to sell the high pasture to people who want to put cabins on it. I have been asked to bring the goats' position, and I do not have one. I have three phrases.

IlluminaraWhy did you send for me?

OdileBecause I am afraid of being wrong in either direction. Call them persons and the assembly may take them from a farm that loves them. Call them stock and I have said exactly what the sale needs me to say. And I will not hand forty living creatures to a court on the strength of what a goat said in a barn.

At the far end of the byre an old nanny with a grey muzzle got up out of the straw, one leg at a time, and came the length of the floor. The others made room without being asked.

BrackenYou are the one who listens. Sit. Sit down. Down in the straw.

Illuminara sat. It seemed to be the first item of business, and the sigh that went through the herd told her she had guessed right.

02 Â· The Count

The byre Â· the second evening

Illuminara spent two evenings in the byre before she asked anyone anything. She had asked Odile to come without her clipboard, and Odile sat on an upturned bucket by the door with an expression somewhere between suspicion and relief.

She ate what the kids ate, which was oat cake pressed with something bitter and green, and found she liked it, and that the kids found her liking it very funny. She learned that the herd did not so much talk as take turns: one word, then a pause long enough to hold a whole thought, then another. She learned that a goat in conversation says the same thing three times, so that nobody has to ask twice. She had also noticed, without quite noticing, that every time one of the three phrases was spoken the whole herd leaned a little toward the east door.

At bedtime the kids played a game. One said a name, the next said it again and added another, and so on down the straw, and a small brown kid with one folded ear always got furthest.

TansyByre Gate. Split Ash. Byre Gate, Split Ash, the Wet Step. Byre Gate, Split Ash, the Wet Step, Tolly's Rise.

IlluminaraWhat is the game called?

TansyThe count.

She said it the way a child says the alphabet. Illuminara sat very still, because something in her head had come loose and she did not want to knock it off the shelf.

Not goats. Places.

IlluminaraTansy, what comes after Tolly's Rise?

Fourteen names came out of the straw in order, ending at a place called the Long Look. The vet had written down three demands. They were one. The hill was where the rhyme went. The count was the rhyme. The going-up was the walking of it.

IlluminaraWho taught you the count?

TansyBracken. Bracken's mother. Bracken's mother's mother. The count.

03 Â· The Registry

The Brannoch Dale registry, back room Â· the fourth morning

The registrar listened to Odile without a word and then wheeled out a flat drawer. The enclosure award of 1844 was a map the colour of weak tea, and across the high ground ran a dotted line labelled in a hand like barbed wire: Common Way, for the going-up and coming-down of beasts. Fourteen waymarks were pencilled along it. Illuminara read them aloud, once, and Odile sat down on a box of ledgers.

OdileSplit Ash. Tolly's Rise. My grandfather said that in the lambing shed. I thought it was a nursery rhyme.

IlluminaraIt is a survey. They have been walking the bounds of their own road for two hundred years and calling it a game, because nobody else was listening.

OdileThen why is it not in Pruett's title?

IlluminaraThe book says a right of common lapses if nobody claims it for twenty years. The herd walks the way every spring. But a goat cannot lodge a claim, so the book records no use.

The registrar slid a second sheet across the desk without comment. It was the sale particulars, offering the high pasture as an unencumbered single lot.

OdileSo I claim the way for the holding, and the sale cannot close as written. But to do that I must say it is the holding's herd that uses it. Which is the word they did not want said. A thing people do to them.

IlluminaraYes. I could draft it tonight, and it would be correct, and it would win. I think we ask first.

OdileAsk whom?

IlluminaraThe people whose road it is.

04 Â· The Correction

The byre Â· that night

She laid it out the slow way, a sentence and then a wait, for as long as it took. The way was in the award. The right belonged to the holding. The cooperative would claim it, the herd's going-up would stand as the use, and forty goats would keep the hill without anyone having to rule on what they were.

It was a good plan. She was a little proud of it, and had not hidden that well. Bracken heard it out to the end. Then she said, three times over, the word Illuminara had hoped not to hear.

BrackenNo. No. No.

IlluminaraTell me where it is wrong.

BrackenFor these forty. Forty die, the way is gone. Not for us. For the kids who are not yet. For whoever the herd is.

Illuminara's pride went out of her all at once, and what came in behind it was more useful. She had written the right for the animals in front of her. The herd had asked for a road that would outlast them.

IlluminaraThe right runs with the herd, whoever it is. Kids born, strays taken in. Not forty names.

BrackenGood. And a hill you are let on is a hill you are let on. We want the hearing. When the way changes, the cooperative counts us first. We say. We say before.

OdileYou want a veto over my own land?

BrackenOver the road. Not the farm. The road is the road.

Odile looked at the old nanny for a long time. Then she looked at the straw, where Tansy lay asleep with her mouth still moving on the count.

OdileIt means the cooperative can never sell the herd's road. Not in a bad year. Not ever.

BrackenNo. And the way must be walked. Every spring. Kids at the front. If it is walked, it is used. If it is used, it is not lost. The hill remembers if we walk.

Illuminara wrote that down and understood what Bracken had just done. The right had lapsed once because nobody could claim it. It would never lapse again, because the claim was a morning walk that the herd would make whether or not anyone ever wrote it in a book.

05 Â· The Gate

The high pasture gate Â· afternoon, four days before the sitting

Mr Hale Pruett was not a villain, which Illuminara found inconvenient. He was a tidy man in a waterproof the colour of porridge, with a client who had paid a deposit, a lender who wanted a clean title, and the look of someone who has read a great many documents carefully and is now being shown one that was not in the file. He had read the award twice.

PruettYou are asking my client to accept, for ever, forty goats walking through his scheme twice a year, on the strength of a nursery rhyme.

IlluminaraOn the strength of an award, which the rhyme matches stone by stone. I walked the line last night with my palm flat on the air. The old groove is still there under the gorse, four hundred years of the same weight on the same line.

PruettThe price falls.

IlluminaraIt does. And the way is in the award whether we tell your lender now or the lender's solicitor tells you in March. This way it is a four-metre lane along the Lamb Wall, outside the cabin plots, disclosed on the first day.

PruettAnd if the goats change their minds?

IlluminaraThen they say so, in the count, to the cooperative, before anything moves. It is the only change to that road you will ever have to deal with, and it will come with notice.

He looked up the hill for a while.

PruettMy guests will ask what the noise is.

IlluminaraTwice a year, a fine one. Bring your lender to the walk.

He did not agree. He wrote spring walk, autumn walk in the margin of the particulars, which is how a careful man agrees without saying so.

06 Â· The Going-Up

The Common Way Â· dawn, the morning of the sitting

They went up in the grey: forty goats, the whole membership of the cooperative and the registrar with a notebook, and Tansy at the front, because the kids go first.

TansyByre Gate, Split Ash, the Wet Step, Tolly's Rise, the Lamb Wall.

At the Wet Step she stopped, put one hoof in the water to prove it was wet, and went on, and forty animals lifted their feet over the same stones in the same order. A hollow the width of a goat ran through the gorse under the frost. Odile walked at the back with her hands in her pockets, saying the names under her breath and getting two of them wrong. Pruett had come, in the porridge-coloured waterproof, with his lender's number on his phone. At the Lamb Wall he stood and looked at the number and did not call it.

Illuminara walked last of all, and was surprised by how much she minded being last. She had decoded a rhyme and found a map and lost an argument to a goat, and none of it had felt like this: a hill being gone up by those it belonged to, one name at a time, at the speed of the smallest.

At the Long Look Bracken stopped, and the herd stopped behind her. The whole dale lay below them in the first sun with its roofs smoking.

BrackenNow the hill knows who is coming.

TansyAgain?

BrackenAgain. Next spring. And the spring after.

07 Â· What the Assembly Left Open

Brannoch Dale assembly hall Â· noon

The assembly's question was whether forty goats were livestock or residents, and it spent an hour discovering that it had not been asked the right one. Odile Marrick stood up with no notes. She said the cooperative was not asking for a category. It was claiming the Common Way in the award of 1844 for its holding, covenanting to hear the herd's chosen speakers before it changed anything, and filing that covenant at the registry with a hoof-print inked beside her signature, because Bracken had insisted on a mark.

The chair asked whether this decided what the goats were. Odile said it decided what they could count on. The assembly adjourned the other question, as she had hoped, and nobody much minded, since the hill was no longer for sale in the form that had made the question urgent. Pruett's client bought the lower meadow and the cabin plots with a lane written into the title, at a lower price and with a lender who asked only that the spring walk be put in the brochure.

It cost something. Two members of the cooperative resigned that week, one of them a man who had wanted the sale money and who would not forgive Odile for choosing the goats' road over his drains. The question of what the herd was remained open, and Bracken's speakers would have to defend the covenant at the count for years.

That night in the byre Tansy said the count, the whole fourteen, and did not lose a name. Illuminara found she was saying it with her, under her breath, the way and not the goats. Odile stood in the doorway with the cooperative's book and no clipboard, and when the last name was done she wrote under the date, in her small square hand, Count taken.

OdileThen why is it not in Pruett's title?

RegistrarBecause the book keeps a right alive by an entry, and nothing else. A holder's claim is an entry. A walk that I or a holder sets down is an entry. Twenty years with none, and the right lapses. The last entry on the Way is nineteen years old, a lambing-week walk my predecessor happened to see.

IlluminaraSo the herd has kept the road alive on foot and let it go on paper. Eleven months left on the paper.

RegistrarAnd a goat cannot lodge a claim. The sale particulars were drawn from the book as it stands.

04 Â· The Correction

The byre Â· that night

BrackenNo. No. No.

BrackenFor these forty. Forty die, the way is gone. Not for us. For the kids who are not yet. For whoever the herd is.

Illuminara's pride went out of her all at once, and what came in behind it was more useful. She had written the right for the animals in front of her. The herd had asked for a road that would outlast them.

IlluminaraThe right runs with the herd, whoever it is. Kids born, strays taken in. Not forty names.

BrackenGood. And a road to a shut gate is no road. The grass at the end of it is the end of the road. We want the hearing. When the way changes, the cooperative counts us first. We say. We say before.

OdileYou want a veto over my own land?

BrackenOver the road. Not the farm. The road is the road.

OdileAnd where it goes?

BrackenA road goes somewhere.

Odile looked at the old nanny for a long time. Then she looked at the straw, where Tansy lay asleep with her mouth still moving on the count.

OdileThen the sale loses the top of the hill. The grass above the Lamb Wall stays in the cooperative's name, out of every lot, and I put that to people who voted for the whole of it. It means the cooperative can never sell the herd's road, or the grass it goes to. Not in a bad year. Not ever.

BrackenNo. And the way must be walked. Every spring. Kids at the front. If it is walked and the book sees it, it is used. If it is used, it is not lost. The hill remembers if we walk.

Illuminara wrote that down and understood what Bracken had just done. The right had come within eleven months of lapsing because nobody could claim it. It would not come near again, because the entry was a morning walk the herd would make whether or not anyone asked, and the registrar had only to be there to see it.

05 Â· The Gate

The high pasture gate Â· afternoon, four days before the sitting

Mr Hale Pruett was not a villain, which Illuminara found inconvenient. He was a tidy man in a waterproof the colour of porridge, acting for a buyer who had paid a deposit, with a lender who wanted a clean title behind her, and the look of someone who has read a great many documents carefully and is now being shown one that was not in the file. IlluminaraOn the strength of an award, which the rhyme matches stone by stone. I walked the line last night with my palm flat on the air. The old groove is still there under the gorse, four hundred years of the same weight on the same line.

PruettAnd the lot is halved.

IlluminaraIt is. The grass above the Lamb Wall leaves the sale. Your client keeps the lower meadow, the road frontage, the water, and seven of his eleven plots. The Way is a four-metre lane along the wall, outside the plots, mapped and registered on the first day instead of found by your lender's solicitor in March.

PruettThe price falls.

IlluminaraIt does. She will lend against a lane she can read. She cannot lend against a claim in the registrar's drawer that nobody has told her about.

PruettAnd if the goats change their minds?

IlluminaraThen they say so, in the count, to the cooperative, before anything moves. It is the only change to that road you will ever have to deal with, and it will come with notice.

PruettMy guests will ask what the noise is.

IlluminaraTwice a year, a fine one. Bring your lender to the walk.

PruettI agree to nothing. I will carry a mapped lane, seven plots and a walk to my client, and they will say what they say.

He wrote spring walk, autumn walk in the margin of the particulars, which is how a careful man marks what he expects to have to defend.

06 Â· The Going-Up

The Common Way Â· dawn, the morning of the sitting

They went up in the grey: forty goats, the whole membership of the cooperative and the registrar with a notebook, and Tansy at the front, because the kids go first.

TansyByre Gate, Split Ash, the Wet Step, Tolly's Rise, the Lamb Wall.

The registrar walked beside the kids and wrote, after each waymark, a line.

Pruett had come, in the porridge-coloured waterproof, with his phone in his hand. At the Lamb Wall he stood and looked at it while the herd went past him, and then he read aloud, to nobody, a message that had come in at five.

PruettFrom my client. Seven good plots and a story no other cabins in the county have, beats eleven plots and a lawsuit. Sign it.

He dialled the other number. Illuminara, last in the line, heard only his half, which was enough: Yes. A registered lane. Seven plots. The ground above the wall out of the sale. A long listening. Forty. The kids go first. Another. He laughed once, unwillingly.

PruettShe will lend against a lane on the title, not against a surprise. And she wants to know whether they walk on Sundays.

Illuminara walked last of all, and was surprised by how much she minded being last. She had decoded a rhyme and found a map and lost an argument to a goat, and none of it had felt like this: a hill being gone up by those it belonged to, one name at a time, at the speed of the smallest.

At the Long Look Bracken stopped, and the herd stopped behind her. The whole dale lay below them in the first sun with its roofs smoking, and the registrar closed his notebook on the first entry of the next twenty years.

BrackenNow the hill knows who is coming.

TansyAgain?

BrackenAgain. Next spring. And the spring after.

07 Â· What the Assembly Left Open

Brannoch Dale assembly hall Â· noon

The assembly's question was whether forty goats were livestock or residents, and it spent an hour discovering that it had not been asked the right one. Odile Marrick stood up with no notes. She said the cooperative was not asking for a category. It was claiming the Common Way in the award of 1844 for its holding, with that morning's walk entered at the registry as use, and covenanting to hear the herd's chosen speakers before it changed the Way or the grass it went to. She had filed the covenant that morning, with a hoof-print inked beside her signature, because Bracken had insisted on a mark.

The chair asked whether this decided what the goats were. Odile said it decided what they could count on. The assembly adjourned the other question, as she had hoped, and nobody much minded, since the hill was no longer for sale in the form that had made the question urgent. The cooperative kept the high pasture above the Lamb Wall in its own name, and the herd would go on grazing it as it always had, on ground the cooperative could not now sell without hearing them first. Pruett's client signed the amendment at the gate before ten: the lower meadow and seven plots, at a lower price, with the lane along the wall written into the title as the Common Way, and a lender who asked only that the spring walk be put in the brochure.

It cost something. Two members of the cooperative resigned that week, one of them a man who had wanted the sale money and who would not forgive Odile for choosing the goats' road over his drains. The question of what the herd was remained open, and Bracken's speakers would have to defend the covenant at the count for years.

That night in the byre Tansy said the count, the whole fourteen, and did not lose a name. Illuminara found she was saying it with her, under her breath, the way and not the goats. Odile stood in the doorway with the cooperative's book and no clipboard, and when the last name was done she wrote under the date, in her small square hand, Count taken.

THE VOTE OF THE UPLIFTED GOATS

Forty goats in a dale byre have voted, unanimously, for something nobody can name, and a sale is about to fence off the hill; Illuminara spends two evenings in the straw until she hears what the vote is a translation of, and lets the herd correct her answer.

01 Â· The Byre

Marrick Farm, Brannoch Dale Â· evening, eleven days before the sitting

The goats had called the meeting, and Odile Marrick wanted that understood before anything else. They had called it by walking into the byre at dusk, all forty, and standing in the straw in the patient way of animals who have listened to people for a long time. A veterinary trial that went wrong eleven years ago had left them able to follow an argument. It had not left them quick about it.

OdileThey voted last Tuesday. Forty on the left side of the byre, and then eleven walked across to the right and back again, and the vet wrote that down as a recount. I think it was a joke.

IlluminaraWhat did they vote for?

Odile held out the vet's notepad. It carried three phrases, copied down over three nights: the hill, the count, the going-up.

OdileThe assembly sits in eleven days to decide whether they are livestock or residents. The cooperative has voted to sell the high pasture, as one lot, through a Mr Pruett, to people who want to put cabins on it. I have been asked to bring the goats' position, and I do not have one. I have three phrases.

IlluminaraWhy did you send for me?

OdileBecause I am afraid of being wrong in either direction. Call them persons and the assembly may take them from a farm that loves them. Call them stock and I have said exactly what the sale needs me to say. And I will not hand forty living creatures to a court on the strength of what a goat said in a barn.

At the far end of the byre an old nanny with a grey muzzle got up out of the straw, one leg at a time, and came the length of the floor. The others made room without being asked.

BrackenYou are the one who listens. Sit. Sit down. Down in the straw.

Illuminara sat. It seemed to be the first item of business, and the sigh that went through the herd told her she had guessed right.

02 Â· The Count

The byre Â· the second evening

Illuminara spent two evenings in the byre before she asked anyone anything. She had asked Odile to come without her clipboard, and Odile sat on an upturned bucket by the door with an expression somewhere between suspicion and relief.

She ate what the kids ate, which was oat cake pressed with something bitter and green, and found she liked it, and that the kids found her liking it very funny. She learned that the herd did not so much talk as take turns: one word, then a pause long enough to hold a whole thought, then another. She learned that a goat in conversation says the same thing three times, so that nobody has to ask twice. She had also noticed, without quite noticing, that every time one of the three phrases was spoken the whole herd leaned a little toward the east door.

At bedtime the kids played a game. One said a name, the next said it again and added another, and so on down the straw, and a small brown kid with one folded ear always got furthest.

TansyByre Gate. Split Ash. Byre Gate, Split Ash, the Wet Step. Byre Gate, Split Ash, the Wet Step, Tolly's Rise.

IlluminaraWhat is the game called?

TansyThe count.

She said it the way a child says the alphabet. Illuminara sat very still, because something in her head had come loose and she did not want to knock it off the shelf.

Not goats. Places.

IlluminaraTansy, what comes after Tolly's Rise?

Fourteen names came out of the straw in order, ending at a place called the Long Look. The vet had written down three demands. They were one. The hill was where the rhyme went. The count was the rhyme. The going-up was the walking of it.

IlluminaraWho taught you the count?

TansyBracken. Bracken's mother. Bracken's mother's mother. The count.

03 Â· The Registry

The Brannoch Dale registry, back room Â· the fourth morning

The registrar listened to Odile without a word and then wheeled out a flat drawer. The enclosure award of 1844 was a map the colour of weak tea, and across the high ground ran a dotted line labelled in a hand like barbed wire: Common Way, as anciently used, for the going-up and coming-down of beasts to the common grazing above the Lamb Wall. Fourteen waymarks were pencilled along it. Illuminara read them aloud, once, and Odile sat down on a box of ledgers.

OdileSplit Ash. Tolly's Rise. My grandfather said that in the lambing shed. I thought it was a nursery rhyme.

IlluminaraIt is a survey. The award only wrote down a road that was already old. They have been saying the bounds of it since before the ink, and calling it a game, because nobody else was listening.

OdileThen why is it not in Pruett's title?

RegistrarBecause there are two ledgers. The award book keeps a right alive by an entry, and nothing else. A holder's claim is an entry. A walk that I or a holder sets down is an entry. Twenty years with none, and the right lapses. Pruett's title is drawn from the parcel index, and the index carries only what a holder has entered against a parcel. The last entry in the award book is nineteen years old, a lambing-week walk my predecessor happened to see. Nobody has ever entered the Way against the land.

IlluminaraSo the right is alive in one book and invisible in the other. The herd has kept the road alive on foot, and the paper has let it go quiet. Eleven months left on the clock.

RegistrarAnd a goat cannot lodge a claim. The sale particulars were drawn from the index as it stands.

He slid a second sheet across the desk. It offered the high pasture as an unencumbered single lot.

OdileSo I enter a claim for the holding, and the Way goes onto the title before the lot is divided, and the sale cannot close as written. But to do that I must say it is the holding's herd that uses it. Which is the word they did not want said. A thing people do to them.

IlluminaraYes. I could draft it tonight, and it would be correct, and it would win. I think we ask first.

OdileAsk whom?

IlluminaraThe people whose road it is.

04 Â· The Correction

The byre Â· that night

She laid it out the slow way, a sentence and then a wait, for as long as it took. The way was in the award. The right belonged to the holding. The cooperative would claim it, the herd's going-up would stand as the use, and forty goats would keep the hill without anyone having to rule on what they were.

It was a good plan. She was a little proud of it, and had not hidden that well. Bracken heard it out to the end. Then she said, three times over, the word Illuminara had hoped not to hear.

BrackenNo. No. No.

IlluminaraTell me where it is wrong.

BrackenFor these forty. Forty die, the way is gone. Not for us. For the kids who are not yet. For whoever the herd is.

Illuminara's pride went out of her all at once, and what came in behind it was more useful. She had written the right for the animals in front of her. The herd had asked for a road that would outlast them.

IlluminaraThe right runs with the herd, whoever it is. Kids born, strays taken in. Not forty names.

BrackenGood. And a road to a shut gate is no road. The grass at the end of it is the end of the road. We want the hearing. When the way changes, the cooperative counts us first. We say. We say before.

OdileYou want a veto over my own land?

BrackenOver the road. Not the farm. The road is the road.

OdileAnd where it goes?

BrackenA road goes somewhere.

Odile looked at the old nanny for a long time. Then she looked at the straw, where Tansy lay asleep with her mouth still moving on the count.

OdileThen the sale loses the top of the hill. The grass above the Lamb Wall stays in the cooperative's name, out of every lot, and I put that to people who voted for the whole of it. I will write more than you asked. Neither the Way nor the grass it goes to can ever be sold, and nothing on either is changed without your speakers heard first. Not in a bad year. Not ever.

BrackenThat is more than we asked. Good. It is not enough. A road no one walks is a road in a drawer. The way must be walked. Every spring. Kids at the front. If it is walked and the book sees it, it is used. If it is used, it is not lost. The hill remembers if we walk.

Illuminara wrote that down and understood what Bracken had just done. The right had come within eleven months of lapsing because nobody could claim it. It would not come near again, because the entry was a morning walk the herd would make whether or not anyone asked, and the registrar had only to be there to see it.

05 Â· The Gate

The high pasture gate Â· afternoon, four days before the sitting

Mr Hale Pruett was not a villain, which Illuminara found inconvenient. He was a tidy man in a waterproof the colour of porridge, acting for a buyer who had paid a deposit, with a lender who wanted a clean title behind her, and the look of someone who has read a great many documents carefully and is now being shown one that was not in the file. He had read the award twice.

PruettYou are asking my client to accept, for ever, forty goats walking through his scheme twice a year, on the strength of a nursery rhyme.

IlluminaraOn the strength of an award, which the rhyme matches stone by stone. I walked the line last night with my palm flat on the air. The old groove is still there under the gorse, four hundred years of the same weight on the same line.

PruettAnd the lot is halved.

IlluminaraIt is. The grass above the Lamb Wall leaves the sale. Your client keeps the lower meadow, the road frontage, the water, and seven of his eleven plots. The Way is a four-metre lane along the wall, outside the plots, mapped and entered on the title on the first day instead of found by your lender's solicitor in March.

PruettThe price falls.

IlluminaraIt does. She will lend against a lane she can read. She cannot lend against a claim in the registrar's drawer that nobody has told her about.

PruettAnd if the goats change their minds?

IlluminaraThen they say so, in the count, to the cooperative, before anything moves. It is the only change to that lane you will ever have to deal with, and it will come with notice.

He looked up the hill for a while.

PruettMy guests will ask what the noise is.

IlluminaraTwice a year, a fine one. Bring your lender to the walk.

PruettI agree to nothing. I will carry a mapped lane, seven plots and a walk to my client, and they will say what they say.

He wrote spring walk, autumn walk in the margin of the particulars, which is how a careful man marks what he expects to have to defend.

06 Â· The Going-Up

The Common Way Â· dawn, the morning of the sitting

They went up in the grey: forty goats, the whole membership of the cooperative and the registrar with a notebook, and Tansy at the front, because the kids go first.

TansyByre Gate, Split Ash, the Wet Step, Tolly's Rise, the Lamb Wall.

At the Wet Step she stopped, put one hoof in the water to prove it was wet, and went on, and forty animals lifted their feet over the same stones in the same order. A hollow the width of a goat ran through the gorse under the frost. Odile walked at the back with her hands in her pockets, saying the names under her breath and getting two of them wrong. The registrar walked beside the kids and wrote, after each waymark, a line.

Pruett had come, in the porridge-coloured waterproof, with his phone in his hand. At the Lamb Wall he stood and looked at it while the herd went past him, and then he read aloud, to nobody, a message that had come in at five.

PruettFrom my client. Seven good plots and a story no other cabins in the county have, beats eleven plots and a lawsuit. Sign it.

He dialled the other number. Illuminara, last in the line, heard only his half, which was enough: Yes. A registered lane. Seven plots. The ground above the wall out of the sale. A long listening. Forty. The kids go first. Another. He laughed once, unwillingly.

PruettShe will lend against a lane on the title, not against a surprise. And she wants to know whether they walk on Sundays.

Illuminara walked last of all, and was surprised by how much she minded being last. She had decoded a rhyme and found a map and lost an argument to a goat, and none of it had felt like this: a hill being gone up by those it belonged to, one name at a time, at the speed of the smallest.

At the Long Look Bracken stopped, and the herd stopped behind her. The whole dale lay below them in the first sun with its roofs smoking, and the registrar closed his notebook on the first entry of the next twenty years.

BrackenNow the hill knows who is coming.

TansyAgain?

BrackenAgain. Next spring. And the spring after.

Uveljavlja Skupno pot iz odloÄbe iz leta 1844 za svoje posestvo; jutranja hoja je bila v knjigo odloÄbe vpisana kot uporaba, zahtevek pa je bil vpisan ob naslovu, preden se je parcela razdelila. THE ELDER WHO WOULD NOT LEAVE THE GRAVES

A barn-sized slab is creeping toward the village of Kesk, and the oldest woman in it has locked herself in the burial ground; Illuminara learns what the refusal is guarding, and the village finds a way to carry it.

01 Â· The Slab

Kesk, the cliff track Â· noon

The slab above Kesk was the size of a barn and had been moving for nine days. It moved the way a tired child leans on a door, so slowly that nobody could say when the leaning had stopped being a lean.

Captain Iven Roth of the valley rescue had called Illuminara himself, which she understood had cost him something. He did not shake her hand. He held out his tablet the way a man shows a doctor a wound he has already diagnosed.

RothTwo millimetres a day on Monday. Eleven an hour this morning. Plot one over the speed and the line walks down to zero at about eight tonight, give or take three hours. Rain is due at eight. I have been telling myself seven.

She laid her palm flat on the cliff beside the track and let the mountain show her where it was holding. The slab did not hang from the face. It leaned on a rock bridge behind it, a column the width of a bus, cracked through like a boiled bone and doing the work of the whole slope. She felt the load run down it the way you feel a pulse in a wrist.

IlluminaraI can take the slab's weight off that bridge. I cannot make the bridge sound, and to hold anything I need two anchors in good rock. This mountain has one place that qualifies: the limestone knuckle where the ledge turns away. Four hours, perhaps.

RothPerhaps.

IlluminaraPlan for three.

He nodded like a man accepting an honest invoice. The track down was two and a half kilometres of ledge, single file, and its first four hundred metres ran under the slab's shadow before the cliff turned away. Sixty-two people lived in Kesk. Sixty-one were on the church green with their bags.

RothThe sixty-second has locked herself in the burial ground. The gate, I mean, with a key. The wall beside it fell down in 'ninety-eight.

02 Â· The Circuit

The burial ground above the church Â· half past twelve

Ostrava Mehl was ninety-one and walked as though the ground were a sentence she was checking for errors. She went along the first row with a stick in one hand and a ring of iron keys in the other, and when Illuminara stepped through the gap where the wall had been, she did not look up.

Illuminara fell in beside her and said nothing for a full row. It was the most useful thing she had done all day. The old woman's lips were moving. She never repeated herself.

A man came up the path at a run and stopped with his hands on his knees. Davor Mehl was fifty, a quarry foreman, and the only person in Kesk who had ever been forgiven for raising his voice to her.

DavorAuntie. The captain says three hours at the outside.

OstravaThen you have time to be quiet.

DavorYou hold mountains, don't you? That is what they say. Then you can hold her. Put her over your shoulder and let us go, and she will thank you at ninety-five.

IlluminaraIt would work. That is what is wrong with it.

Ostrava stopped, for the first time, and looked at Illuminara properly, as one inspects a stranger who has said something unexpected.

IlluminaraWhat are you saying?

OstravaNames. Two hundred and fourteen, in the order they lie. Thirty-seven have no stone: the cough winter, the Pirc fire, the ones nobody could pay a mason for. There was a book. The school burned in 'seventy-four and the book went with it, and my mother gave me these keys and the order of the rows and said, whoever walks this after you walks it from you or not at all. I have been through it twice since the siren. I lose the order at the east wall.

IlluminaraSo you are not refusing to leave.

OstravaI am refusing to leave them unsaid.

IlluminaraHow long does it take to say them all?

OstravaFour hours, walking. I will not say a name I have not stood at.

DavorAuntie, nobody asked you that.

OstravaNobody asked me anything. They asked if I was coming. I did the sum myself, Davor, before you had your boots on. Four hours, and the track is an hour and a half. I chose the names.

Illuminara saw then that the village had spent the morning arguing with a refusal, and that no one had asked what it was for. Davor had gone the colour of the church wall. She let him.

03 Â· Three Clocks

The church porch Â· one in the afternoon

They laid it out on the porch flags with Roth's tablet and a stub of builder's chalk, because it turned out to be three problems wearing one coat.

IlluminaraThe slab's clock: down by eight at the latest, sooner if the rain is early. The track's clock: three hours for sixty-two people, with the old and the stones. Ostrava's clock: four hours, because the list can only travel as fast as one pair of legs.

OstravaLegs are what a person has.

IlluminaraThen borrow more. The list is not slow. The walking is. Sit you in a chair at the crossing of the paths. Six people each take a row, walk to a grave, and stand at it. You say who lies there, from where you sit. A seventh writes. They have stood at the place, and you have heard them stand. Two hours, perhaps less.

OstravaAnd when they stand at the wrong mound?

IlluminaraThen you tell them, and they go to the right one. That is all a reading is.

OstravaThey will point at moles.

RothEveryone who is not needed for the reading goes now, children first, and the bend belongs to them alone until they are across.

RothYour hold starts when the slab tells us, not when it suits you. Anchors are a clock too. Four hours spent at noon are four hours you will not have at six.

DavorThe geophone is mine. I listen to the cracking. Forty ticks a minute and we go, and whatever is unfinished stays where it is. Nobody argues.

IlluminaraYour call, not mine. If it crosses forty, I am not an argument.

OstravaCan you put it down somewhere else?

IlluminaraNo. It falls where the plane tilts. I can choose when. The when has to be after you are all out.

IlluminaraThe twelve line stones, the first stone of each row, are the oldest and the heaviest. I can lift them down the ledge myself.

OstravaYou will not. They go by sledge, on shoulders. Whoever carries them down will carry them again on All Souls. A stone that arrived by miracle is a stone nobody has to look after.

She was right, and she had made it a better plan. Illuminara wrote sledges, shoulders on the flags and underlined it.

OstravaDavor. You will carry the register.

DavorAuntie, I will carry you.

OstravaNot me. What I say. Lift that, and four of your neighbours may carry the rest of me, on the Pirc chair, because my own has a bad leg.

For a moment Davor looked as though he might refuse the whole arrangement out of pure relief. Then he took the tablet from Roth and held it as one holds something alive.

04 Â· The Reading

The limestone knuckle at the far end of the ledge Â· half past two

The gauge touched twenty millimetres an hour at twenty-seven minutes past two, and Illuminara set both hands into the only sound rock on the mountain and took the slab's weight onto her own.

It was like lifting a table that a hundred people were leaning on. Roth stood beside her with a handset clipped to his collar and a face that offered nothing, which she was grateful for. Through the radio, from three hundred metres up the slope, came a kitchen chair scraping on gravel.

Ostrava (radio)Row one, from the church wall. Hedvig Mehl, who kept bees. Josef Brunec, who did not. Mara Pirc, four years old. Tomo, you are on her feet. Step back one.

Davor (radio)Written. Next.

Up the slope, through the bridge and into the shoulder, she could feel what the mountain remembered of two hundred and fourteen openings, each a small softness where hands had once dug and filled. She could have counted them. She could not have told one from another by anything but its depth, which was the whole point of the old woman and the whole trouble.

The names came across the gorge like a train of small carriages, each with someone in it. Row two. Row three.

Ostrava (radio)That is a mole, Brina.

The radio filled with six people trying not to laugh.

Ostrava (radio)East wall. Third mound.

The radio hissed. A minute went, and another. Under Illuminara's hands the north anchor shifted a hair, like a tooth.

Ostrava (radio)I had it this morning.

Davor (radio)Nan Pirc's boy. Teodor. He fell off the Pirc barn in 'fifty-one and Nan hauled a bench up here every Sunday till she died.

Ostrava (radio)Who told you that?

Davor (radio)You did. I was nine. You made me carry the bench.

There was a long hiss. Illuminara held the mountain and waited.

Ostrava (radio)Write it.

It was, she thought, the first name Ostrava had said in nine hours that she had not said alone.

05 Â· The Bend

The far end of the ledge Â· ten to six

They came along the ledge in the last of the light, twenty-two lanterns in a line against a face the colour of slate: six sledges with the line stones lashed on, a chain of neighbours who had done this every All Souls of their lives, and in the middle, four of them carrying a kitchen chair with a very small woman sitting bolt upright in it. Davor walked at the back with the tablet inside his coat and the geophone in his hand.

Ostrava was reciting. It had turned out that the register was in four-four time, and the bearers had fallen into step with it, a name to a stride, so that the chair rode level on the ledge.

At twenty to six the rock under Illuminara's left hand turned to grit.

IlluminaraRoth. North anchor is gone. I am moving east. Eleven minutes at half strength.

RothSay it to Davor.

IlluminaraDavor. Eleven minutes, half strength.

Davor (radio)Thirty-four.

She slid her hand along the knuckle, found a seam, and leaned into it. The slab took a breath above them, and she felt it as a stranger's weight on her arm.

Davor (radio)Thirty-eight.

The lanterns were two hundred metres from her, with two hundred still to cross. Forty, she thought, and the plan says run. She did not speak. It was his line.

Davor (radio)We are past the middle and forward is shorter. Walk. Do not run. Running shakes the ledge.

Down the gorge came the old woman's voice, flat and steady, at the pace of a walk.

Ostrava (radio)Alois Vrh, a good man and a bad fiddler. Lena Vrh, who forgave him.

Davor (radio)Thirty-six.

At eleven minutes the seam held. The first sledge reached her, and the man hauling it said something to nobody that had the shape of a prayer, and kept going. The chair passed. Ostrava did not look at her. She was on row nine.

Davor (radio)Last foot clear.

IlluminaraThen I am putting it down.

RothSay it to someone who can answer.

IlluminaraOstrava. Is there anything the mountain still owes you?

Ostrava (radio)Nothing it can pay.

She lowered it. She did not drop it. For a moment the slab seemed to remember it was falling, as a person remembers a name, and then it went, and the shoulder opened and closed over two hundred and fourteen small softnesses all at once. She did not look away. The slab came down on empty ground.

06 Â· East Corner

The threshing terrace below Kesk Â· the next morning, frost

Sixty-two people stood on the old threshing terrace with their breath hanging over them, and above them the mountain had a new face. Nobody said the word cairn. Nobody would for a year.

Davor had three printed copies of the register in a plastic sleeve: one for the diocese, one for the council, one for the cousins in Toronto, who had rung every All Souls and been told, correctly, that Aunt Rozalija lay at the top of the hill in the third row, and who would ring this year and be told, correctly, that she lay at the east corner of the lower terrace in the third row, and that her name would be cut in April. Twelve line stones stood in a row on the frosted grass. Beside Illuminara a woman called Brina, who had been wrong about the moles, put a cup of tea into her hands without asking. They shook a little. She let them.

DavorThe council sat till two. The thirty-seven are to be cut first, before any stone that already had one.

OstravaI would have asked for that.

DavorYou did not ask.

OstravaI did not think it could be asked.

She stood up from the Pirc chair without help, took her stick in one hand and her keys in the other, and went along the terrace until she had found the corner she wanted. She put the Mehl line stone down herself and took her time over it.

OstravaEast corner. It gets the sun in the morning.

OstravaYou told me you could not put it down somewhere else.

IlluminaraI could not.

OstravaThen all you did was let it fall when we were ready. I do not forgive the mountain. I may forgive you by spring.

Davor, kneeling in the frost, wrote the corner's name at the top of a clean page.
