RothYour hold starts when the slab tells us, not when it suits you. Anchors are a clock too. Four hours spent at noon are four hours you will not have at six.

RothThen the ledge. The old trough stands two hundred metres in, under the slab. Short of the trough, if anything goes wrong, you turn back to the kiln cut: two metres of rock over it, and I have sat under it myself. Past the trough, the way out is forward. Nobody runs. A running line shakes the ledge.

DavorThe geophone is mine. I listen to the cracking. Forty ticks a minute, or Illuminara tells me an anchor is going, and we do what the captain said, and whatever is unfinished stays where it is. Nobody argues.

IlluminaraYour call, not mine. If it crosses forty, I am not an argument.

DavorAnd the sum, so I do not invent it on the day. Two hundred metres at a chair's pace is five minutes, six on a bad step. If she gives me double that, we go on.

OstravaCan you put it down somewhere else?

IlluminaraNo. It falls where the plane tilts. I can choose when. The when has to be after you are all out.

IlluminaraThe twelve line stones, the first stone of each row, are the oldest and the heaviest. I can lift them down the ledge myself.

OstravaYou will not. They go by sledge, on shoulders. Whoever carries them down will carry them again on All Souls. A stone that arrived by miracle is a stone nobody has to look after.

She was right, and she had made it a better plan. Illuminara wrote sledges, shoulders on the flags and underlined it.

OstravaWhen the reading is done I will say them all again, from the chair, going down. Once to the ground, once to the six who stood on it.

OstravaDavor. You will carry the register.

DavorAuntie, I will carry you.

OstravaNot me. What I say. Lift that, and four of your neighbours may carry the rest of me, on the Pirc chair, because my own has a bad leg.

For a moment Davor looked as though he might refuse the whole arrangement out of pure relief. Then he took the tablet from Roth and held it as one holds something alive.

04 Â· The Reading

The limestone knuckle at the far end of the ledge Â· half past two

The gauge touched twenty millimetres an hour at twenty-seven minutes past two, and Illuminara set both hands into the only sound rock on the mountain and took the slab's weight onto her own.

It was like lifting a table that a hundred people were leaning on. Roth stood beside her with a handset clipped to his collar and a face that offered nothing, which she was grateful for. Through the radio, from three hundred metres up the slope, came a kitchen chair scraping on gravel.

Ostrava (radio)Row one, from the church wall. Hedvig Mehl, who kept bees. Josef Brunec, who did not. Mara Pirc, four years old. Tomo, you are on her feet. Step back one.

Davor (radio)Written. Next.

Up the slope, through the bridge and into the shoulder, she could feel what the mountain remembered of two hundred and fourteen openings, each a small softness where hands had once dug and filled. She could have counted them. She could not have told one from another by anything but its depth, which was the whole point of the old woman and the whole trouble.

The names came across the gorge like a train of small carriages, each with someone in it. Row two. Row three.

Ostrava (radio)That is a mole, Brina.

The radio filled with six people trying not to laugh.

Ostrava (radio)East wall. Third mound.

The radio hissed. A minute went, and another. Under Illuminara's hands the north anchor shifted a hair, like a tooth.

Ostrava (radio)I had it this morning.

Davor (radio)Nan Pirc's boy. Teodor. He fell off the Pirc barn in 'fifty-one and Nan hauled a bench up here every Sunday till she died.

Ostrava (radio)Who told you that?

Davor (radio)You did. I was nine. You made me carry the bench.

There was a long hiss. Illuminara held the mountain and waited.

Ostrava (radio)Write it.

It was, she thought, the first name Ostrava had said in nine hours that she had not said alone.

At twenty past four the chair scraped for the last time. Row twelve was said, and the thirty-seven with no stone were pegged where the walkers had stood, each peg tied with a name.

Ostrava (radio)The count.

Davor (radio)Two hundred and fourteen. Thirty-seven pegs, and Brina has counted them twice. Every one is on the tablet against its row and its paces from the east wall.

Ostrava (radio)Read me the last peg.

Davor (radio)Jelka Pirc. The Pirc fire. Row twelve, three paces from the east wall.

RothThe valley station has the file. A second copy, off the mountain.

Ostrava (radio)Then it is out of my head. Good. It was getting heavy.

05 Â· The Bend

The far end of the ledge Â· ten to six

They came along the ledge in the last of the light, twenty-two lanterns in a line against a face the colour of slate: six sledges with the line stones lashed on, a chain of neighbours who had done this every All Souls of their lives, and in the middle, four of them carrying a kitchen chair with a very small woman sitting bolt upright in it. Davor walked at the back with the tablet inside his coat and the geophone in his hand.

Ostrava was saying them again, as she had promised, from row one. It had turned out that the register was in four-four time, and the bearers had fallen into step with it, a name to a stride, so that the chair rode level on the ledge.

At twenty to six the rock under Illuminara's left hand turned to grit.

IlluminaraRoth. North anchor is gone. I am moving east. Eleven minutes at half strength.

RothSay it to Davor.

IlluminaraDavor. Eleven minutes, half strength.

Davor (radio)Thirty-four.

She slid her hand along the knuckle, found a seam, and leaned into it. The slab took a breath above them, and she felt it as a stranger's weight on her arm.

Davor (radio)Thirty-eight.

The lanterns had just cleared the trough, two hundred metres from her, with two hundred still to cross. Forty, she thought, and the plan says the kiln cut or on. She did not speak. It was his line.

Davor (radio)We are past the trough, and forward is six minutes against her eleven. Walk. Do not run. Running shakes the ledge.

Down the gorge came the old woman's voice, flat and steady, at the pace of a walk.

Ostrava (radio)Alois Vrh, a good man and a bad fiddler. Lena Vrh, who forgave him.

Davor (radio)Thirty-six.

At eleven minutes the seam held. The first sledge reached her, and the man hauling it said something to nobody that had the shape of a prayer, and kept going. The chair passed. Ostrava did not look at her. She was on row nine.

Davor (radio)Last foot clear.

IlluminaraThen I am putting it down.

RothSay it to someone who can answer.

IlluminaraOstrava. Is there anything the mountain still owes you?

Ostrava (radio)Nothing it can pay.

She lowered it. She did not drop it. For a moment the slab seemed to remember it was falling, as a person remembers a name, and then it went, and the shoulder opened and closed over two hundred and fourteen small softnesses all at once. She did not look away. The slab came down on empty ground.

06 Â· East Corner

The threshing terrace below Kesk Â· the next morning, frost

Sixty-two people stood on the old threshing terrace with their breath hanging over them, and above them the mountain had a new face. Nobody said the word cairn. Nobody would for a year.

Davor had three printed copies of the register in a plastic sleeve: one for the diocese, one for the council, one for the cousins in Toronto. They had rung every All Souls and been told, correctly, that Aunt Rozalija lay at the top of the hill in the third row. This year they would be told, correctly, that she lay there still, under the mountain, and that her name would be cut in April at the east corner of the terrace below, where the living could stand. The register had two columns now, because Ostrava had insisted: where each of them lies, and where each is remembered. Twelve line stones stood in a row on the frosted grass. Beside Illuminara a woman called Brina, who had been wrong about the moles, put a cup of tea into her hands without asking. They shook a little. She let them.

DavorThe council sat till two. The terrace is a memorial ground, not a graveyard. Nobody is moved, and nobody is to say they were. The thirty-seven are to be cut first, before any stone that already had one.

OstravaI would have asked for that.

DavorYou did not ask.

OstravaI did not think it could be asked.

She stood up from the Pirc chair without help, took her stick in one hand and her keys in the other, and went along the terrace until she had found the corner she wanted. She put the Mehl line stone down herself and took her time over it.

OstravaEast corner. It gets the sun in the morning.

OstravaYou told me you could not put it down somewhere else.

IlluminaraI could not.

OstravaThen all you did was let it fall when we were ready. I do not forgive the mountain. I may forgive you by spring.

Davor, kneeling in the frost, wrote the corner's name at the top of a clean page.

THE ELDER WHO WOULD NOT LEAVE THE GRAVES

A barn-sized slab is creeping toward the village of Kesk, and the oldest woman in it has locked herself in the burial ground; Illuminara learns what the refusal is guarding, and the village finds a way to carry it.

01 Â· The Slab

Kesk, the cliff track Â· noon

The slab above Kesk was the size of a barn and had been moving for nine days. It moved the way a tired child leans on a door, so slowly that nobody could say when the leaning had stopped being a lean.

Captain Iven Roth of the valley rescue had called Illuminara himself, which she understood had cost him something. He did not shake her hand. He held out his tablet the way a man shows a doctor a wound he has already diagnosed.

RothTwo millimetres a day on Monday. Eleven an hour this morning. Plot one over the speed and the line walks down to zero at about eight tonight, give or take three hours. Rain is due at eight. I have been telling myself seven.

She laid her palm flat on the cliff beside the track and let the mountain show her where it was holding. The slab did not hang from the face. It leaned on a rock bridge behind it, a column the width of a bus, cracked through like a boiled bone and doing the work of the whole slope. She felt the load run down it the way you feel a pulse in a wrist.

IlluminaraI can take the slab's weight off that bridge. I cannot make the bridge sound, and to hold anything I need two anchors in good rock. This mountain has one place that qualifies: the limestone knuckle where the ledge turns away. Four hours, perhaps.

RothPerhaps.

IlluminaraPlan for three.

He nodded like a man accepting an honest invoice. The track down was two and a half kilometres of ledge, single file, and its first four hundred metres ran under the slab's shadow before the cliff turned away. Sixty-two people lived in Kesk. Sixty-one were on the church green with their bags.

RothThe sixty-second has locked herself in the burial ground. The gate, I mean, with a key. The wall beside it fell down in 'ninety-eight.

02 Â· The Circuit

The burial ground above the church Â· half past twelve

Ostrava Mehl was ninety-one and walked as though the ground were a sentence she was checking for errors. She went along the first row with a stick in one hand and a ring of iron keys in the other, and when Illuminara stepped through the gap where the wall had been, she did not look up.

Illuminara fell in beside her and said nothing for a full row. It was the most useful thing she had done all day. The old woman's lips were moving. She never repeated herself.

A man came up the path at a run and stopped with his hands on his knees. Davor Mehl was fifty, a quarry foreman, and the only person in Kesk who had ever been forgiven for raising his voice to her.

DavorAuntie. The captain says three hours at the outside.

OstravaThen you have time to be quiet.

DavorYou hold mountains, don't you? That is what they say. Then you can hold her. Put her over your shoulder and let us go, and she will thank you at ninety-five.

IlluminaraIt would work. That is what is wrong with it.

Ostrava stopped, for the first time, and looked at Illuminara properly, as one inspects a stranger who has said something unexpected.

IlluminaraWhat are you saying?

OstravaNames. Two hundred and fourteen, in the order they lie. Thirty-seven have no stone: the cough winter, the Pirc fire, the ones nobody could pay a mason for. There was a book. The school burned in 'seventy-four and the book went with it, and my mother gave me these keys and the order of the rows and said, whoever walks this after you walks it from you or not at all. I have been through it twice since the siren. I lose the order at the east wall.

IlluminaraSo you are not refusing to leave.

OstravaI am refusing to leave them unsaid.

IlluminaraHow long does it take to say them all?

OstravaFour hours, walking. I will not say a name I have not stood at.

DavorAuntie, nobody asked you that.

OstravaNobody asked me anything. They asked if I was coming. I did the sum myself, Davor, before you had your boots on. Four hours, and the track is an hour and a half. I chose the names.

Illuminara saw then that the village had spent the morning arguing with a refusal, and that no one had asked what it was for. Davor had gone the colour of the church wall. She let him.

03 Â· Three Clocks

The church porch Â· one in the afternoon

They laid it out on the porch flags with Roth's tablet and a stub of builder's chalk, because it turned out to be three problems wearing one coat.

IlluminaraThe slab's clock: down by eight at the latest, sooner if the rain is early. The track's clock: three hours for sixty-two people, with the old and the stones. Ostrava's clock: four hours, because the list can only travel as fast as one pair of legs.

OstravaLegs are what a person has.

IlluminaraThen borrow more. The list is not slow. The walking is. Sit you in a chair at the crossing of the paths. Six people each take a row, walk to a grave, and stand at it. You say who lies there, from where you sit. A seventh writes. They have stood at the place, and you have heard them stand. Two hours, perhaps less.

OstravaAnd when they stand at the wrong mound?

IlluminaraThen you tell them, and they go to the right one. That is all a reading is.

OstravaThey will point at moles.

RothEveryone who is not needed for the reading goes now, children first, and the bend belongs to them alone until they are across.

RothYour hold starts when the slab tells us, not when it suits you. Anchors are a clock too. Four hours spent at noon are four hours you will not have at six.

RothThen the ledge. The old trough stands two hundred metres in, under the slab. Short of the trough, if anything goes wrong, you turn back to the kiln cut: two metres of rock over it, and I have sat under it myself. Past the trough, the way out is forward. Nobody runs. A running line shakes the ledge.

DavorThe geophone is mine. I listen to the cracking. Forty ticks a minute, or Illuminara tells me an anchor is going, and we do what the captain said, and whatever is unfinished stays where it is. Nobody argues.

IlluminaraYour call, not mine. If it crosses forty, I am not an argument.

DavorAnd the sum, so I do not invent it on the day. From the trough to the knuckle is two hundred metres. A chair's pace covers that in five minutes, six on a bad step, and I want half as much again in hand for the last foot. Nine. At the trough I ask her for her hold. Nine or more, we pass it. Less, we take the kiln cut.

OstravaCan you put it down somewhere else?

IlluminaraNo. It falls where the plane tilts. I can choose when. The when has to be after you are all out.

IlluminaraThe twelve line stones, the first stone of each row, are the oldest and the heaviest. I can lift them down the ledge myself.

OstravaYou will not. They go by sledge, on shoulders. Whoever carries them down will carry them again on All Souls. A stone that arrived by miracle is a stone nobody has to look after.

She was right, and she had made it a better plan. Illuminara wrote sledges, shoulders on the flags and underlined it.

OstravaWhen the reading is done I will say them all again, from the chair, going down. Once to the ground, once to the six who stood on it.

OstravaDavor. You will carry the register.

DavorAuntie, I will carry you.

OstravaNot me. What I say. Lift that, and four of your neighbours may carry the rest of me, on the Pirc chair, because my own has a bad leg.

For a moment Davor looked as though he might refuse the whole arrangement out of pure relief. Then he took the tablet from Roth and held it as one holds something alive.

Ostrava (radio)Alois Vrh, a good man and a bad fiddler. Lena Vrh, who forgave him.

Davor (radio)Thirty-six.

RothSix minutes.

2026-10-19S3 Â· THE BED BETWEEN THEM

T01 Â· AUTHOR_DRAFT

Full English original Â· celotno angleÅ¡ko besedilo Â· Published story lineage / objavljena veja

Distinct authentic author text preserved from the source event.

THE BED BETWEEN THEM

A dying officer will not speak, and a mother needs a grave. Illuminara looks for a door narrow enough for both of them to walk through.

01 Â· The Ward at Midnight

Saint Aldis Hospice, east ward Â· first night, past midnight

Eleven beds stood where the ward had been built for seven, and the lamp at the nurses' table was turned down to a bead. The man in the bed by the window had not spoken in three weeks. The woman in the next bed, kept in for a lung fever, had stopped pretending to sleep an hour ago.

Maren Teague was reading the card clipped to the foot of his bed. It said OSTROW, H., and under it, in a clerk's hand, late Ninth Militia.

Something stood at the foot of that bed that had not been there when the lamp was turned down. It did not look like a visitor. It looked like attention that had found a shape: a tall woman with the stillness of someone who has learned to wait for the end of a sentence.

IlluminaraI will go if you would rather I did not stay. I only come where someone is awake and willing to see me.

Maren did not answer at once. She had spent eleven years among clerks who said they were sorry.

MarenYou are not a nurse.

IlluminaraNo. I listen. Sometimes that turns out to be the only skill a night needs.

MarenThen listen to this. The Ninth held the ford the winter my son went missing. That card says Ninth. I have written to the registry eleven times and they tell me the records of the ford were burned. And here he lies, an arm's length from me, breathing. He knows where my boy is, and he will not say.

She said it quietly, as if the man might hear. Illuminara glanced at him. His eyes were open, fixed on the ceiling, and a muscle moved in his jaw.

IlluminaraWhat do you want from him?

MarenA place. Anything else I could learn to live without. A place, so I can stop standing in every field.

IlluminaraThen I should tell you what I won't do, before you start to hope for it. I won't make him speak. I won't carry a word between you that he has not allowed. If I did, I would be a knife, and you would never know what he gave and what was taken.

MarenThen what use are you?

IlluminaraI don't know yet. I would like to find out whether there is a door. Silence like his is often a wall. Sometimes it is a shield, and a shield can have a way through it.

A shield held for three weeks, the whole arm trembling, Illuminara thought. Someone is standing behind it who is not him.

MarenAnd if there is no door?

IlluminaraThen I will tell you so, and you will have lost a night, not a year.

Maren lay back. Across the narrow gap between the beds, Halvard Ostrow's hand lay open on the blanket, and for the first time she looked at it as the hand of a man rather than of a regiment.

02 Â· The Shield

Saint Aldis Hospice, east ward Â· second night, before dawn

She went to the other bed this time. Halvard Ostrow's eyes moved from the ceiling to her face and stayed there, which no one on the ward had ever reported him doing.

IlluminaraI will ask you nothing tonight. I wanted you to know I am here, and that you may send me away.

He took a long time to move his mouth. His voice, when it came, had the dryness of paper kept too long.

HalvardYou are not the chaplain. The chaplain wants my sins. You want something else.

IlluminaraThe woman beside you wants a place. She has not asked me to take it from you. She asked what I could do, and I told her I could not make you speak.

HalvardShe is Teague's mother.

It was not a question. Illuminara waited.

HalvardI knew whose mother she was the day they wheeled her in. I read the chart. Pell Teague. A scout, quick on his feet. I wrote his name on the list myself, and then the list burned.

IlluminaraYou remember him.

HalvardI remember all eleven. That is the trouble with silence. It keeps better than paper.

He turned his face to the wall, and she thought she had lost him. Then:

HalvardYou want me to give her a grave. A grave is a place, and a place had a burial party, and a burial party has a roll. I sent one of that party away from the line before the ford broke. I told her to walk north and not look back. If I name the ground, the tribunal at Corvane asks who dug it. If they ask who dug it, they will find who was not at the muster. You want me to give a grave and in exchange hang my child. I will take my silence to my own.

He had said it without raising his voice, and the words stood in the room like furniture.

IlluminaraI will not argue with that. It is a reasonable fear, and I cannot promise you the tribunal's mind.

He is not guarding the dead, she thought. He is guarding the living, and the dead are the price he has been paying.

HalvardThen we are finished.

IlluminaraNot quite. I have been listening to what you actually said. You said a place has a burial party. You did not say a place has a name. A grave is not a person. What would you need, to be able to speak the ground without the roll?

For the first time he looked at her as at a colleague rather than a visitor.

HalvardSomeone who finds it who is not the tribunal. Someone with no reason to ask how it came to be there. And the woman cannot be asked to name what she was never told.

Illuminara did not answer. She had the beginning of a shape, and she held it lightly, because it was still his to accept or refuse.

03 Â· What Maren Was Asked

Saint Aldis Hospice, east ward Â· third night, the lamp low

Illuminara sat on the edge of Maren's chair, the way one sits when the news is not all good.

IlluminaraI have a proposal. Before I tell you its shape I will tell you what it costs you. Then you may refuse.

MarenTell me.

IlluminaraHe will tell you one thing: where your son lies, and how to know the ground. He will not tell you how it came to be, or who else was there, or why he stayed silent. He has a reason that is not yours to hold, and he asks that you not chase it. You would have a place and no full account.

Maren said nothing for so long that the ward's breathing seemed to grow louder.

MarenNo.

IlluminaraSay why. Say it fully. I would rather hear your objection than your politeness.

MarenBecause you are asking me to take a grave and let the man who dug it die clean. Eleven years I have been owed an account. Who gave the order, who stood by, who put my boy in a hole. And you tell me to trade that for a patch of ground and be grateful. It is not a bargain. It is a bribe in the shape of a mercy.

IlluminaraIt is also exactly what it is. I will not dress it. He dies with his reasons, and some of what you are owed stays unpaid. You may refuse, and I will tell him so, and nothing is lost between you that was not already lost.

If I argued her round, Illuminara thought, I would take the one thing she has left: that the choice is hers.

MarenDoes it free him?

IlluminaraNo. It does not absolve him, and nothing said tonight will. If you ever petition the registry for the rest, that claim stays yours. You give up nothing you may lawfully ask. You only agree not to press him for it, here, now.

Maren turned toward the gap between the beds. Halvard's eyes were open.

MarenA place, and nothing about him.

IlluminaraA place. And he asks nothing of your claims. Only that you do not make him speak of the rest.

Footsteps. Nurse Ines Varga came down the ward with the lamp and stopped, having heard the end of it. She was a broad-shouldered woman who had closed more eyes than she cared to count, and she did not pretend she had heard nothing.

InesI'll tell you what I can promise and what I can't. What is said between these two beds I will not write in the ward book. That much is mine to keep. But if a magistrate's writ comes for this ward, I cannot lie to it, and neither can you, Mrs. Teague. Don't ask me for more than I have.

MarenI understand.

IlluminaraThen here is the door. Halvard says he can speak the ground if the one who finds it is no part of the tribunal. Ines, is there anyone here who buries the dead and asks nothing beyond the ground?

InesTobias Rell. He digs for the parish and for us. He'd go. He doesn't talk.

IlluminaraThen I carry one question, once, to each of you, and I carry nothing else. Halvard, do you permit it?

A long pause.

HalvardCarry it. I promise nothing past the ground.

IlluminaraMaren?

MarenAsk him where my son is. Only that.

Illuminara turned to the other bed and gave the question in Maren's own words, no softer and no sharper. Halvard closed his eyes.

HalvardThe ford. North bank. Count the posts of the drowned fence from the willow. At the fourth, six paces toward the river. A flat stone, no marker. Eleven lie there. He is the one under the stone, because I knew his name.

He opened his eyes and turned them on the woman in the next bed, and spoke to her directly for the first time in three weeks.

HalvardI am sorry for the stone. It was all I had.

MarenIt is not enough.

HalvardNo.

MarenBut it is a place.

04 Â· The Stone Under the Willow

Saint Aldis Hospice, yard and east ward Â· fourth day and night

Tobias Rell left at first light with a spade, a lantern and a boy to hold the horse. He came back at noon, mud to the knees, and stood in the ward door with his cap in his hands.

TobiasFourth post, six paces. Flat stone. Under it a boy in a militia cloak, and a tin with a tag. Notched along the rim.

Maren had told Illuminara about the notches the night before: three, then two, then one, cut with her own kitchen knife the week he enlisted, so that she would know him if he were ever found among others. She had said it to no one else. She said it now, and Tobias nodded, and she sat down on the bed.

TobiasThere's ten more in the trench beside him. I've told the parish clerk I found an old grave of eleven at the ford and entered the tags I could read. Their families will want to know.

Illuminara looked at Halvard. He had heard. His face did not change, but the hand on the blanket closed.

HalvardA grave found by a keeper is a grave found. I cannot stop that, and I will not ask it.

That was the cost, plainly stated, Illuminara thought. The ledger now held a trench of eleven, and a ledger can be asked questions.

IlluminaraThe parish will wonder who buried them. I cannot promise it will not follow that road. I can only say the road now begins at a found grave and not at your voice.

HalvardThat is more than I had.

They buried Pell Teague at dusk in the hospice yard, under the pear tree, because the village plot was a day's ride away and Maren would not wait another night. Ines read the words. Tobias set the stone. Maren said nothing at all, and then said his name once, and the ground took it.

Afterwards she did not go straight back inside. She stood by the new turf with Illuminara.

MarenI still want the rest.

IlluminaraYou may still ask for it. The registry is open to you. I did not take that.

MarenI know. I think that is why I could say yes.

She went in. In the east ward the lamp was turned low. Halvard Ostrow was awake and had been waiting for her, and for a while neither of them spoke.

HalvardYou may sit.

MarenI will. I do not forgive you.

HalvardI did not ask it.

She sat on the edge of her own bed, a hand's breadth from his, and kept the vigil with him anyway, not for his sake or her own but because it was the fourth night and the ward was full of people who should not be alone.

Before dawn his breathing stopped. He had said his one true thing and kept the other. Illuminara stood at the foot of the bed until the nurse came, and then went the way she had come.

Somewhere north, a daughter walked under another name, owing a debt she did not know had been paid. In Corvane a ledger held a trench of eleven that someone might yet ask about. Maren Teague had a grave to visit and a question she would carry for the rest of her life. Neither was a mercy. Both were real.

THE BED BETWEEN THEM

A dying officer will not speak, and a mother needs a grave. Illuminara looks for a door narrow enough for both of them to walk through.

01 Â· The Ward at Midnight

Saint Aldis Hospice, east ward Â· first night, past midnight

Eleven beds stood where the ward had been built for seven, and the lamp at the nurses' table was turned down to a bead. The man in the bed by the window had not spoken in three weeks. The woman in the next bed, kept in for a lung fever, had stopped pretending to sleep an hour ago.

Maren Teague was reading the card clipped to the foot of his bed. It said OSTROW, H., and under it, in a clerk's hand, late Ninth Militia.

Something stood at the foot of that bed that had not been there when the lamp was turned down. It did not look like a visitor. It looked like attention that had found a shape: a tall woman with the stillness of someone who has learned to wait for the end of a sentence.

IlluminaraI will go if you would rather I did not stay. I only come where someone is awake and willing to see me.

Maren did not answer at once. She had spent eleven years among clerks who said they were sorry.

MarenYou are not a nurse.

IlluminaraNo. I listen. Sometimes that turns out to be the only skill a night needs.

MarenThen listen to this. The Ninth held the ford the winter my son went missing. That card says Ninth. I have written to the registry eleven times and they tell me the records of the ford were burned. And here he lies, an arm's length from me, breathing. He knows where my boy is, and he will not say.

She said it quietly, as if the man might hear. Illuminara glanced at him. His eyes were open, fixed on the ceiling, and a muscle moved in his jaw.

IlluminaraWhat do you want from him?

MarenA place. Anything else I could learn to live without. A place, so I can stop standing in every field.

IlluminaraThen I should tell you what I won't do, before you start to hope for it. I won't make him speak. I won't carry a word between you that he has not allowed. If I did, I would be a knife, and you would never know what he gave and what was taken.

MarenThen what use are you?

IlluminaraI don't know yet. I would like to find out whether there is a door. Silence like his is often a wall. Sometimes it is a shield, and a shield can have a way through it.

A shield held for three weeks, the whole arm trembling, Illuminara thought. Someone is standing behind it who is not him.

MarenAnd if there is no door?

IlluminaraThen I will tell you so, and you will have lost a night, not a year.

Maren lay back. Across the narrow gap between the beds, Halvard Ostrow's hand lay open on the blanket, and for the first time she looked at it as the hand of a man rather than of a regiment.

02 Â· The Shield

Saint Aldis Hospice, east ward Â· second night, before dawn

She went to the other bed this time. Halvard Ostrow's eyes moved from the ceiling to her face and stayed there, which no one on the ward had ever reported him doing.

IlluminaraI will ask you nothing tonight. I wanted you to know I am here, and that you may send me away.

He took a long time to move his mouth. His voice, when it came, had the dryness of paper kept too long.

HalvardYou are not the chaplain. The chaplain wants my sins. You want something else.

IlluminaraThe woman beside you wants a place. She has not asked me to take it from you. She asked what I could do, and I told her I could not make you speak.

HalvardShe is Teague's mother.

It was not a question. Illuminara waited.

HalvardI knew whose mother she was the day they wheeled her in. I read the chart. Pell Teague. A scout, quick on his feet. I wrote his name on the burial slip myself. The company roll burned, but the tribunal kept the order book at Corvane: dates, officers and the five soldiers assigned to each burial detail.

IlluminaraOne of those five was your daughter.

HalvardLysa. I sent her north before the ford broke and signed that the detail had been completed. If I give Corvane my name, the ground and that day, I join the three things their book keeps apart. They ask for the five. They find four. They hang the fifth for desertion and me for the lie.

He turned his face to the wall. When he spoke again, each word had already been tried against silence.

HalvardYou want me to give a grave and in exchange hang my child. I will take my silence to my own.

IlluminaraThat fear is coherent. I cannot promise you the tribunal will never find the grave, or that Lysa is safe. May I ask what the book does not contain?

He looked at her then. She had not disputed the danger or renamed it cowardice.

HalvardNo place. No names of the dead. No description of the ground. The orders were numbered by date. The burials were not.

His voice is the missing join, Illuminara thought. The place can be found without the join, but not without risk.

IlluminaraThen your testimony would make the road short. A parish finder who discovers an old trench can give families names without giving Corvane the date, the officer or the burial detail. The tribunal could still investigate. A writ could still reach this ward. But the grave itself would not supply the link only you can supply.

HalvardAnd how does a finder discover a stone six paces from a drowned fence?

IlluminaraBy receiving directions without your name and testing them in daylight with witnesses. The parish records what is under the ground before anything is moved. The ward records no confession unless the law requires it. Those are not walls. They are only distance. Is distance enough for you to consider the question?

HalvardIf the finder writes every dead man he can identify. If he does not ask who dug. If she keeps her right to ask elsewhere. I will not buy her silence about me.

IlluminaraThose are conditions, not consent. I will carry them as conditions. Nothing more.

For the first time he looked at her as at a colleague rather than a visitor. He had not agreed. He had changed the shape of what agreement would require.

03 Â· What Maren Was Asked

Saint Aldis Hospice, east ward Â· third night, the lamp low

Illuminara sat on the edge of Maren's chair, the way one sits when the news is not all good.

IlluminaraI have a proposal. Before I tell you its shape I will tell you what it costs you. Then you may refuse.

MarenTell me.

IlluminaraHe will give directions to a parish finder without giving his name, the date or the burial party. The finder will test the place and record what is there. He will not give you the full account. His reason is a living person whose danger I cannot promise away. You would have a place and no full answer.

Maren said nothing for so long that the ward's breathing seemed to grow louder.

MarenNo.

IlluminaraSay why. Say it fully. I would rather hear your objection than your politeness.

MarenBecause you are asking me to take a grave and let the man who made it die clean. Eleven years I have been owed an account. Who gave the order, who stood by, who put my boy in a hole. You tell me to trade that for a patch of ground and be grateful. It is not a bargain. It is a bribe in the shape of a mercy.

IlluminaraIt is also exactly what it is. I will not dress it. Some of what you are owed stays unpaid. You may refuse, and I will tell him so, and nothing is lost between you that was not already lost.

If I argued her round, Illuminara thought, I would take the one thing she has left: that the choice is hers.

Maren looked across the gap. Halvard did not turn away. His hand had closed around the edge of the blanket.

MarenIf I say yes, these are my terms. The finder marks the ground before he lifts a bone. He brings a parish witness. Every readable tag goes into the book, not only Pell's. My petition to the registry stays mine. No one lies if a lawful writ comes here.

IlluminaraYes.

MarenAnd I choose the proof before he goes. Pell carried a tin tag I marked myself. I will write the marks and fold the paper. The finder must say what he saw before it is opened.

The words cost her. She had to stop once before she could ask Nurse Ines for paper.

Ines Varga came down the ward with the lamp. She was a broad-shouldered woman who had closed more eyes than she cared to count. Maren wrote, folded the slip twice and pressed it beneath the wax of Ines's medicine seal.

InesI will keep this until the finder reports. I will not put bedside speech in the ward book. That much is within my charge. If a magistrate brings a lawful writ, I will not lie, and I will not hide the seal. That is the limit of what I can promise.

MarenThat is enough promise.

IlluminaraHalvard, you heard her terms and Ines's limit. The independent record may help families and may give Corvane a road it does not have tonight. Your name and the date will not be carried, but no one can guarantee they stay hidden. Do you permit one question?

A cart rattled past in the street and was gone. Halvard watched Maren hold the sealed paper against her palm.

HalvardAsk it.

IlluminaraMaren?

Maren looked at the folded slip, then at the man in the next bed.

MarenAsk him where my son is. Only that. I keep the rest.

Illuminara gave the question in Maren's own words, no softer and no sharper. Halvard took one breath and then another.

HalvardThe ford. North bank. Count the posts of the drowned fence from the willow. At the fourth, six paces toward the river. A flat stone, no marker. The trench is beside it. Pell is under the stone because I knew his name.

He turned to Maren and spoke to her directly for the first time in three weeks.

HalvardI am sorry for the stone. It was all I had.

MarenIt is not enough.

HalvardNo.

MarenBut if the marks answer, it may be a place.

04 Â· The Stone Under the Willow

Saint Aldis Hospice, yard and east ward Â· fourth day and night

Tobias Rell left at first light with the parish clerk, a spade, measuring cord and a board for notes. Maren remained in the ward. The sealed slip remained in Ines's apron. No one asked Halvard another question.

They returned at noon, mud to the knees. Tobias stood in the ward door with his cap in his hands; the clerk held a page signed by both of them.

TobiasFourth post, six paces. Flat stone. We measured before we lifted it. Under it was a young man in a militia cloak, beside a trench of ten. We sketched the lay and copied every tag before moving anything. His tin tag has six cuts: three close, a space, two close, a space, one.

Ines broke her medicine seal and handed the folded slip to the clerk. He read it once, turned it so Maren could see, and laid it beside the field page. Three. Two. One.

Maren sat down on the bed. Her hand found the empty place at her throat where the kitchen knife had hung before she gave it to her son.

Parish clerkThe marks agree. Pell Teague is the man under the stone. Five other tags are readable. Four are damaged. One has none. I have entered all of them as found at an old battlefield grave. The place, the bodies and the tags are witnessed. No burial order, officer or detail is named in what we found.

MarenAnd the others?

Parish clerkThe named families will be told. The unknown will remain in the parish book until someone can answer for them. Corvane may ask how the grave came to be. If it sends a writ, we answer what the law asks.

Halvard had heard. His hand closed on the blanket and opened again.

HalvardYour book gives them a place and not my date.

Parish clerkIt gives what two witnesses saw. Nothing less and nothing invented.

HalvardThen write it.

The clerk signed. So did Tobias. The risk had not vanished; Halvard had chosen the smaller road with its end still out of sight.

At dusk, after the parish had authorized the removal of the identified body, they buried Pell Teague in the hospice yard under the pear tree. Maren would not wait another night for the village plot. Ines read the words. Tobias set the stone. The clerk placed a copy of the field page in Maren's hand.

Maren said her son's name once. Then she read aloud the five other names that could be read, because her condition had not been only for her own dead.

Afterwards she stayed by the new turf with Illuminara while the others went inside.

MarenI still want the rest.

IlluminaraYou kept the right to ask for it.

MarenYou did not give me the grave.

IlluminaraNo. He gave the ground. You made the terms. Tobias and the clerk proved it.

MarenYou heard the join he was afraid of.

IlluminaraI heard what he did not need to give. It left room for the rest of you to act.

They returned to the east ward. The lamp was low. Halvard Ostrow was awake and had been waiting for Maren.

MarenThe paper says Pell Teague. It also says five other names, four damaged tags and one man with none. I will take that book to the registry. I will not promise where the asking ends.

HalvardI did not buy that promise.

MarenNo. You did not.

She sat on the edge of her own bed, a hand's breadth from his. She did not forgive him. He did not ask. She kept the vigil because it was the fourth night and the ward was full of people who should not be alone.

Before dawn his breathing stopped. Ines closed his eyes, then wrote his death in the ward book and nothing that had not happened.

In the parish ledger, the found trench had six names, five unfinished lines and no burial detail. Corvane might yet ask for the missing join. Maren had a grave to visit and a witnessed page with which to keep asking. Neither was a mercy. Both were real.

Illuminara carried no route with her; there was none yet. Tobias came with mud still dried on his boots, the clerk with a blank field board instead of a ledger.

I keep no second note naming a source I never met. If a magistrate puts me under lawful writ, I will say Illuminara carried the location. I cannot say who told her.

THE BED BETWEEN THEM

Saint Aldis Hospice, east ward Â· first night, past midnight

Eleven beds stood where the ward had been built for seven, and the lamp at the nurses' table was turned down to a bead. The man by the window had not spoken in three weeks. The woman in the next bed, kept in for a lung fever, had stopped pretending to sleep an hour ago.

Maren Teague was reading the card clipped to his bed. It said OSTROW, H., and under it, in a clerk's hand, late Ninth Militia.

Something stood at the foot of the bed that had not been there when the lamp was turned down. It looked like attention that had found a shape: a tall woman with the stillness of someone who had learned to wait for the end of a sentence.

IlluminaraI will go if you would rather I did not stay. I only come where someone is awake and willing to see me.

Maren did not answer at once. She had spent eleven years among clerks who said they were sorry.

MarenYou are not a nurse.

IlluminaraNo. I listen. Sometimes that turns out to be the only skill a night needs.

MarenThen listen. The Ninth held the ford the winter my son went missing. I have written to the registry eleven times, and they tell me the records burned. Here lies a Ninth officer, an arm's length from me, breathing. He knows where my boy is, and he will not say.

She said it quietly, as if the man might hear. His eyes were open, fixed on the ceiling, and a muscle moved in his jaw.

IlluminaraWhat do you want from him?

MarenA place. Anything else I could learn to live without. A place, so I can stop standing in every field.

IlluminaraThen I should tell you what I won't do. I won't make him speak. I won't carry a word between you that he has not allowed. If I did, I would be a knife, and you would never know what he gave and what was taken.

MarenThen what use are you?

IlluminaraI don't know yet. I would like to find out whether there is a door. Silence like his is often a wall. Sometimes it is a shield, and a shield can have a way through it.

A shield held for three weeks, the whole arm trembling, Illuminara thought. Someone is standing behind it who is not him.

MarenAnd if there is no door?

IlluminaraThen I will tell you so, and you will have lost a night, not a year.

Maren lay back. Across the narrow gap, Halvard Ostrow's hand lay open on the blanket, and for the first time she looked at it as the hand of a man rather than of a regiment.

02 Â· The Shield

Saint Aldis Hospice, east ward Â· second night, before dawn

Illuminara went to the other bed. Halvard's eyes moved from the ceiling to her face and stayed there, which no one on the ward had reported him doing.

IlluminaraI will ask you nothing tonight. I wanted you to know I am here, and that you may send me away.

His voice, when it came, had the dryness of paper kept too long.

HalvardYou are not the chaplain. The chaplain wants my sins. You want something else.

IlluminaraThe woman beside you wants a place. She has not asked me to take it from you.

HalvardShe is Teague's mother. I knew it when they wheeled her in. Pell was a scout. I wrote his name on the burial slip. The company roll burned, but Corvane kept the order book: dates, officers and the five soldiers assigned to each detail.

IlluminaraOne of those five was your daughter.

HalvardLysa. I sent her north before the ford broke and signed that the detail had been completed. The Ninth is no secret. Teague's petitions have put it everywhere Corvane looks. The secret is the day. The tags carry marks, not dates. The book keeps details by date and no burial places. Give Corvane the ground and the day, and it finds me. It asks for five and finds four. It hangs the fifth for desertion and me for the lie.

He turned his face to the wall.

HalvardYou want me to give a grave and in exchange hang my child. I will take my silence to my own.

IlluminaraThat fear is coherent. I cannot promise Lysa is safe. But a parish finder could test a place without learning the day, the officer or the detail. The ground could give families names without giving Corvane the join its book requires.

HalvardAnd how does a finder discover a stone six paces from a drowned fence?

IlluminaraBy receiving directions without your name and testing them in daylight with witnesses. A lawful writ could still reach this ward. Distance is not a wall. Is it enough for you to consider the question?

HalvardIf every readable tag goes into the book. If the finder does not ask who dug. If she keeps her right to ask elsewhere. I will not buy her silence about me.

IlluminaraThose are conditions, not consent. I will carry them as conditions. Nothing more.

For the first time he looked at her as at a colleague rather than a visitor. He had not agreed. He had changed the shape of what agreement would require.

03 Â· What Maren Was Asked

Saint Aldis Hospice, east ward Â· third night, the lamp low

Illuminara sat on the edge of Maren's chair, the way one sits when the news is not all good.

IlluminaraHe will give a parish finder directions without his name or the day. The finder will test the place and record every body and tag. You would have a place and no full account. A living person may be safer, but I cannot promise that either.

MarenNo.

IlluminaraSay why. I would rather hear your objection than your politeness.

MarenBecause you are asking me to take a grave and let the man who made it die clean. Eleven years I have been owed an account: who ordered it, who stood by, who put my boy in a hole. A patch of ground in exchange is a bribe in the shape of a mercy.

IlluminaraIt is also exactly what it is. I will not dress it. Some of what you are owed stays unpaid. You may refuse.

If I argued her round, Illuminara thought, I would take the one thing she has left: that the choice is hers.

Maren looked across the gap. Halvard did not turn away.

MarenIf I say yes, these are my terms. The finder marks the ground before he lifts a bone. He brings a parish witness. Every readable tag goes into the book. My petition stays mine. No one lies if a lawful writ comes here.

IlluminaraYes.

MarenAnd I choose the proof. Pell carried a tin tag I marked myself. I will write the marks and seal them. The finder must say what he saw before they are opened.

Nurse Ines brought paper and her medicine seal. Maren wrote, folded the slip twice and pressed it beneath the wax.

InesI will keep this until the finder reports. Bedside speech does not enter my ward book. But if a magistrate brings a lawful writ, I will not lie or hide the seal.

MarenThat is enough promise.

Halvard's fingers opened at ward book and closed again at writ. Illuminara understood that he was measuring not secrecy but the length of the road to Lysa.

Maren sent for Tobias Rell and the parish clerk. Beyond the ward doors, she put her own name on the search. The clerk listened, then held up his blank field board.

Parish clerkI can write that Tobias tested a location supplied by a private bearer at Maren Teague's request. The field page records the ground, bodies and tags. It records no day that the ground cannot prove and names no source I never met. Under lawful writ, I say Illuminara carried the place. Nothing less and nothing invented.

Illuminara carried that wording back once. Halvard listened with Corvane's book in his imagination.

HalvardThe Ninth is already in her petitions. What Corvane lacks is the day. The trench cannot give it, and your page will not guess it.

Parish clerkIt will not.

HalvardIf Corvane asks whether I spoke?

InesWithout a writ, I do not turn hospice speech into parish evidence. With one, I say that you spoke. I will not invent the words, and I will not deny the fact.

HalvardI did not buy that promise.

IlluminaraNo. You have heard where the distance ends. Do you permit one question?

Halvard studied the blank board, then the seal in Maren's palm.

HalvardAsk it.

IlluminaraMaren?

MarenAsk him where my son is. Carry only the place. I keep the rest.

Illuminara gave the question in Maren's own words, no softer and no sharper.

HalvardNorth bank of the ford. Count the posts of the drowned fence from the willow. At the fourth, six paces toward the river. A flat stone. Pell is under it because I knew his name.

He turned to Maren.

HalvardI am sorry for the stone. It was all I had.

MarenIt is not enough.

HalvardNo.

MarenBut if the marks answer, it may be a place.

04 Â· The Stone Under the Willow

Saint Aldis Hospice, yard and east ward Â· fourth day and night

At first light Illuminara carried the route to Tobias exactly once. He left with the clerk, a spade, measuring cord and a board for notes. Maren remained in the ward. The sealed slip remained in Ines's apron. No one asked Halvard another question.

They returned at noon, mud to the knees.

TobiasFourth post, six paces. Flat stone. We measured before we lifted it. Under it was a young man in a militia cloak beside a trench of ten. His tin tag has six cuts: three close, a space, two close, a space, one.

Ines broke her seal. The clerk opened the slip only after Tobias had finished, laid it beside the field page and signed across both. Three. Two. One.

Maren sat down. Her hand found the empty place at her throat where the kitchen knife had hung before she gave it to her son.

Parish clerkThe marks agree. Pell Teague is under the stone. Five other tags are readable, four damaged, one absent. The page records the ground, the bodies and the tags. It supplies no burial date.

MarenAnd the others?

Parish clerkThe named families will be told. The unknown remain in the book until someone can answer for them.

Halvard had heard. His hand closed on the blanket and opened again.

HalvardThen sign it.

The clerk and Tobias signed.

At dusk, after the parish authorized the removal, they buried Pell in the hospice yard under the pear tree. Maren would not wait another night for the village plot. Ines read the words. Tobias set the stone. The clerk placed a copy of the field page in Maren's hand.

Maren said her son's name once. Then she read aloud the five other names, because her condition had not been only for her own dead.

Afterwards she stayed by the new turf with Illuminara while the others went inside.

MarenI still want the rest.

IlluminaraYou kept the right to ask for it.

MarenYou did not give me the grave.

IlluminaraNo. Halvard gave the ground. You made the terms. Tobias and the clerk proved it.

MarenYou heard the join he was afraid of.

IlluminaraI listened until fear became precise enough to leave a door beside it.

They returned to the east ward. Halvard was awake and waiting.

MarenThe page says Pell Teague. It also says five other names, four damaged tags and one man with none. I will take it to the registry. I will not promise where the asking ends.

HalvardI did not buy that promise.

MarenNo. You did not.

She sat on the edge of her bed, a hand's breadth from his. She did not forgive him. He did not ask. She kept the vigil because it was the fourth night and the ward was full of people who should not be alone.

Before dawn his breathing stopped. Ines closed his eyes, then wrote his death in the ward book and nothing that had not happened.

In the parish ledger, six names stood above five unfinished lines. Maren kept the page open.

THE STAR THAT ONLY SPOKE IN FLARES

A ring habitat of fourteen thousand has seventy hours to decide whether its star is attacking it, and the only evidence that it is not is a silence nobody had thought to measure.

01 Â· Seventy Hours

Calloway Reach, Ring Council chamber Â· Day 1, 07:10

The clock on the chamber wall read 70:00:00. Someone had put it there so that it would be read aloud, and nobody had. Outside the long window the ring turned slowly under a red sun the size of a coin, with fourteen thousand people on its inner face, and the room smelled of cold tea and of a council that had not slept.

Illuminara had come aboard three weeks earlier on an ice tender, hired by the Rakel cooperative to model how heat and light loaded the cutting quays. She was in the chamber because her models were the only ones nobody had yet argued with. She stood at the back, beside the water jug.

Teodora VanceThe Wick has thrown six bursts in eleven days, each one larger than the last. Each came after our evening traffic peaked, and each came in a pattern the shield office calls structured. The forecast for the seventh is one point three times the rating of our parasol. Ferries need twenty-six hours to load. The order has to go out by hour forty-two.

Pell RourkeThe parasol takes one burst. I will not promise it takes that one.

IlluminaraWhat does leaving cost?

Teodora VanceRakel. Everything we mine. A ring that leaves carries eight months of water, and after that it's a very large cup.

Vance was sixty and square-shouldered, with the habit of putting the worst number first so that no one could accuse her of hiding it. She turned from the window to face the back of the room.

Teodora VanceDon't tell me it's talking. We have no way to know it is speaking at all, and you are asking fourteen thousand people to stay under a possible weapon on the strength of a pattern. If I'm wrong about the weapon we lose the ice and a year. If you're wrong we lose the ring.

IlluminaraI won't tell you it's talking. I haven't seen enough to say so, and you would be right not to believe me if I did. I'd like your dock-clock traffic log, Dr. Brannock's flare record, and the room until the evening session.

Teodora VanceThe council sits at eighteen hundred. At eighteen hundred I put the evacuation to a vote.

IlluminaraThen I'll bring you something you can test before you vote.

Vance looked at the clock and then at her, and for a moment the worst-number-first habit gave way to something nearer curiosity.

Teodora VanceTest it with what? We have sixty-nine hours and fifty-one minutes.

02 Â· Nine Years of Margins

Ring observatory, Brannock's office Â· Day 1, 09:30

Ilse Brannock kept the flare record in a ledger as well as on the network, because the ledger could not be edited by anyone who disliked it. Nine years of the Wick filled it, night by night, in a small upright hand with notes in the margins. Some of the notes were questions. A few were underlined twice.

Ilse BrannockYou're the ice woman with the thermal models.

IlluminaraToday I'm the ice woman who read your flare record. It's very clean. Who keeps it?

Ilse BrannockI do. Nobody else wanted to. A flare is a flare.

IlluminaraYou don't think so.

Ilse put a hand flat on the ledger as one might on a sleeping animal.

Ilse BrannockI showed this to two people. One laughed, kindly, which was worse. I said the Wick grows louder when we do. After every busy evening the next burst is larger by about the same fraction. A star's flares don't care about our trade. I knew how it sounded, so I stopped saying it.

IlluminaraAnd the timing?

Ilse BrannockAlways the small hours. I assumed the burst followed the peak and took its time. Stars are slow.

Illuminara laid the traffic log over the flare record on the glass table. The two were kept on different clocks, the observatory's on station mean time and the exchange net's on dock time, three minutes apart. Nobody had ever held both together, because the net belonged to the communications office and carried private trade.

She slid one three minutes. The bright marks on Ilse's record dropped into the troughs of the traffic like coins into a purse.

Ilse BrannockFour-oh-five. That's the shift change. The whole ring goes quiet for twenty minutes while the night crews swap.

IlluminaraIt isn't the loud that it answers, Doctor. It's the quiet after the loud. The louder we were, the bigger it is, and it only ever speaks when we stop.

Ilse BrannockSay what that sounds like.

IlluminaraSomeone raising their voice over a crowd and waiting for a gap. It is exactly the sentence that gets a woman laughed at, and I'd rather it didn't have to be mine. So tell me how I'm wrong.

Ilse took the question the way a hungry person takes bread. She stood, went to the window, and counted on her fingers without looking at them.

Ilse BrannockOne. A spot group turning every twenty-four-point-one hours would fire at four-oh-five for weeks, and I'd see a ring-shaped coincidence. Two. The lull could be the effect. A flare's precursors scrub our band and the traffic drops because the star dimmed it. Three. Three nights on three instruments is three nights.

IlluminaraThen we do something a habit can't do, and neither can a spinning spot or a precursor. We fill the old gap with traffic and open a new one three hours later, on purpose, and write down what each of you predicts first.

Ilse BrannockYou want me at the council.

IlluminaraI want the person who has listened for nine years to say what it has heard and what she isn't sure of. I'll sit behind you.

03 Â· The Case for Flight

Council chamber Â· Day 1, 18:00

Vance opened the session by reading the clock aloud: fifty-nine hours, ten minutes. Then she gave the floor to Rourke, who stood with his hands behind him, like a man giving evidence in a trial he hoped to lose.

Pell RourkeSeventh burst, ordinary traffic, one point three of rating. The parasol is nine hundred metres of foil on a spine. We can edge it on to the sun and it will take more than it's built for, once. It will not be the same plate afterward, and after it comes an eighth.

Teodora VanceThat's the case for flight, and I will make it myself so that nobody thinks I haven't. Evacuations fail when they start late. A star that gets louder every time we speak is a danger whether or not it means anything by it. I would rather lose the ice than argue about intent with fourteen thousand lives on the table.

Gideon Farrow rose from the guild benches before she had finished sitting. He was round and beautifully dressed and had been counting something in his head for the whole of the session.

Gideon FarrowThen I ask the other question. Who pays if we flee from a weather event? The ice is my guild's wages and the ring's drinking water. It can't be that nobody's allowed to ask whether the star is just a star.

Teodora VanceNobody has forbidden it. Dr. Brannock?

Ilse stood. Her hands were steady on the lectern, and Illuminara, three rows back, saw her find the notes she had meant not to need.

Ilse BrannockI have nine years of flares and three nights checked on three instruments with three clocks. The timing sits in the quiet of our traffic, and the size follows the loudness before it. I have three rivals, and I can't kill them with what I have. A spot turning at twenty-four hours. A lull that is an effect rather than a cause. And plain chance.

Teodora VanceIn other words you don't know.

Ilse BrannockIn other words I know where I could be wrong, Chair. That's most of what I know.

Illuminara rose last and did not go to the lectern.

IlluminaraI can't tell this council that the star is a mind. I can tell you that the data prefers one explanation to two others, and that there's a measurement that costs nothing and kills my explanation if I'm wrong. At four-oh-five tomorrow we fill the shift change with ordinary traffic. At seven we pause the exchange net for twenty-two minutes. If the star is a spot, it fires at four-oh-five. If the lull is its effect, our band fails then. If it is answering us, it fires in the new gap. We write the three predictions down tonight and seal them.

Gideon FarrowAnd what does that twenty-two minutes cost my traders?

IlluminaraTell me what they need to keep, and I'll try to leave it alone. What would you protect?

Gideon FarrowThe settlement window. Nothing clears in a pause. Keep the pause off the settlement hour and I'll sell it to the guild myself.

IlluminaraSeven is clear of it. Madam Chair, the cancel line is yours to write. I'll ask that it not be mine.

Vance took a pen to a sheet of paper, which was a thing nobody in the chamber had seen done in years.

Teodora VanceAny burst over nine-tenths of rating, any burst outside both windows, or any failure of the net at four-oh-five. Any one of them and the ferries load within the hour, and nobody argues with me. The manifests are cut tonight and the ferries are fuelled whatever you find. I vote at hour forty.

04 Â· The Gap Moves

Communications office, exchange net Â· Day 2, 06:52

At four-oh-five the old gap had been filled, and the Wick had said nothing at all. Nadim Oyelaran told them so with a flat voice and a face that was trying not to be asked about it. The net had run at full tilt through the shift change, and the band had stayed clean, with no precursor and no scrubbing. Ilse had been awake since the previous morning and sat very straight in a chair she had not chosen.

Nadim OyelaranSealed envelope is in the council log. Three lines. Rotation says four-oh-five, which didn't happen. The effect-not-cause rival says our band fails at four-oh-five, which it didn't. Your line says seven, within twenty-five minutes either side, at between a half and seven-tenths of rating.

Ilse BrannockSay it again about the amplitude.

Nadim OyelaranLast night's dusk peak was a little under average. Half to seven-tenths.

At seven the exchange net went quiet. It was not a failure and not a maintenance. It was a request, read over the ring's speakers by a Chair who did not like making requests. Kitchens went on, ferries stayed fuelled, and the trading floor went still for twenty-two minutes as fourteen thousand people agreed to stop talking over something they could not hear.

Illuminara watched the photometer on the wall. It was a flat line, then a flat line, and then it was not.

It arrived at seven-oh-two, after the old four-oh-five hour had gone by unanswered. It rose in a sharp stroke and decayed over forty minutes, with the kind of long shoulder on the descent that Ilse had marked in the margin of her ledger for nine years with a small question mark.

Ilse BrannockFifty-eight hundredths.

Nadim OyelaranThat's inside the envelope.

Nobody cheered. Ilse took off her glasses and put them down on the console, and Illuminara found, with a surprise that she let show, that she was holding the older woman's elbow.

Ilse BrannockIt came late for its own clock and on time for ours.

IlluminaraThat's one night, Doctor. I'd like a second before anyone calls it anything.

Ilse BrannockOf course you would. So would I. Do you know, it's the first time I've wished for a second night that wasn't to catch myself out.

05 Â· Forty Hours

Council chamber Â· Day 2, 23:10

The second gap had been smaller. It was eighteen minutes at half past ten, announced on the ring's speakers and in the children's nets as a quiet game, with the guild's blessing and Gideon's reluctant arithmetic. The Wick had fired at ten thirty-six, at thirty-one hundredths of rating, against a forecast of a quarter to four-tenths. Clock hour forty found the chamber very full and unnaturally silent.

Teodora VanceTwo nights. Two predictions, written first, both inside their windows, neither rival close. I'll tell you what I won't say. I won't say it's a mind. I'll say that it behaves like a thing that answers our schedule, and a thing that answers our schedule can be planned around. That's a different problem from the one I was voting on at eighteen hundred yesterday.

Pell RourkeIt's a problem I can work. I can edge the parasol before the burst and not after. Planned, the load falls to about a third.

Teodora VanceMy objection stands, though. Suppose we speak to it and we are wrong about what we're saying. Suppose we insult it.

IlluminaraWe might. I can't promise we won't. What I can do is make the first thing we say the thing it has been waiting for. A quiet that we announce ourselves is the first word of an answer, before any signal. If the star hears nothing else, it hears that we stopped.

Teodora VanceAnd if it doesn't hear that?

IlluminaraThen it fires at an hour of its own choosing, over nine-tenths, and your cancel line sends the ferries. Nothing about the line changes. You keep the right to stop me, Chair, until the last second.

Vance put her pen down. She had hold of the sheet with the cancel line on it, and she smoothed it flat, the way people do before they sign something.

Teodora VanceThe ferries stay fuelled. The evacuation is cancelled, not the line. I'll put that to the council as a vote, and anyone who wants to leave on their own account may take a berth. Is there a second?

Dov Achterberg, who had spoken only once in two days, seconded it in a tone that implied he was seconding a motion to be wrong in public. It passed, nine to two. The two did not look away, and Vance did not ask them to.

Afterwards the reply was designed on a console in the communications office at one in the morning. Nadim had the ice-cutting laser, a forty-metre array that spent its life dissolving comet in the Rakel belt. Ilse had her ledger open to the long decay at the end of each flare.

Nadim OyelaranI can give it a beam. I can't tell you it'll see it.

IlluminaraNeither can I. I know it listens in the band we crowd. The laser is the second line. The silence is the first.

Ilse BrannockThen nothing in it that says anything. It shouldn't speak first and it shouldn't speak long. Three pulses on the rhythm of its own rising edge, and then silence for as long as the pulses took. Heard, and your turn.

IlluminaraThat's the whole of it?

Ilse BrannockIt's a great deal of it. I've wanted for nine years to say that much to someone who might be listening.

06 Â· A Turn at Four-Oh-Five

Calloway Reach, whole ring Â· Day 4, 04:05

The largest burst was forecast for four-oh-five. Under ordinary traffic Pell Rourke's figure was one point three of rating. After the guild had agreed to cap its dusk peak by a third, which cost it the most profitable evening of the quarter, it was eight-tenths, plus or minus a seventh. The parasol stood edge-on to the sun, turned at the cost of a month's thruster propellant, and the Rakel quays had been left unshielded because there was no way to protect everything.

The ring went quiet at four-oh-one. It was the shift change, and in four minutes the night crews would hand over to the day. There was no trade on the floor, and no stream on the children's nets, and the long corridors of the inner face filled with fourteen thousand people holding still. In the communications office Nadim held the beam array a hand's breadth from firing and did not look at anyone.

Teodora VanceAny burst over nine-tenths and the line stands.

Pell RourkeUnderstood, Chair.

The three pulses went out at four-oh-five, and then the ring did what the star had never been given in nine years, which was to leave a space.

The flare arrived at four-oh-nine. It rose in its sharp stroke and the photometer screamed, and for forty seconds Illuminara could not make out the numbers on the wall. Then Ilse said the figure aloud, and it was seventy-four hundredths.

Pell RourkeLoad on the plate is a third of that. We're holding.

They were holding. They lost two relay masts at Rakel Quay to the burst, and they would later discover that the guild had lost more trade in one evening than it earned in some months. No one was hurt. In the long decay that followed, with the beam array still silent and the ring still holding its breath, the photometer drew a shape Ilse had never seen in the whole of her ledger. It was three small steps, one after another, like someone knocking.

Ilse BrannockThree. It's three. I have nine years and it has never made three before.

IlluminaraDon't read it yet. We don't know that it's a reply.

Ilse BrannockNo. We don't know.

The second night they did it again, with the same quiet, the same three pulses, and a smaller burst at the right hour. The photometer drew the three steps once more, in the same places, a little smaller as the flare was.

Nobody said anything for a time. Gideon Farrow, who had come to the office to settle a bill, stood in the doorway with the bill in his hand, and did not hand it to anyone.

Teodora VanceDaily window. Forty minutes at the shift change, for as long as the council keeps voting for it, and I'll hold the cancel line myself. Dov's side won't believe a word of this, and I won't ask them to. I'll ask them to keep the silence.

Ilse BrannockDo you want to know what it said?

IlluminaraI want to know whether it says it again. If it does, then perhaps one day we'll learn what it means. Until then I'm glad someone has been listening for nine years.

Ilse closed the ledger and then, after a moment, opened it again, and wrote on the next clean line with a small upright hand: Four-oh-five. We stopped. It knocked.

THE STAR THAT ONLY SPOKE IN FLARES

A ring habitat of fourteen thousand has seventy hours to decide whether its star is attacking it, and the only evidence that it is not is a silence nobody had thought to measure.

Teodora VanceThe Wick has thrown six dangerous bursts in eleven days, against a nine-year background of one every forty-four hours. They cluster after our evening traffic peaks. The next natural alignment is due near dawn on Day Four. Under ordinary traffic the shield office forecasts one point three times the rating of our parasol. Ferries need twenty-six hours to finish loading. The order has to go out by hour forty-two.
